A Proof of Weak Cosmic Censorship Conjecture for the Spherically Symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-Charged Scalar Field System
Xinliang An, Hong Kiat Tan

Abstract
. Under spherical symmetry, we show that the weak cosmic censorship holds for the gravitational collapse of the Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field system. Namely, for this system, with generic initial data, the formed spacetime singularities are concealed inside black-hole regions. This generalizes Christodoulou’s celebrated results to the charged case. Due to the presence of charge Q Q and the complexification of the scalar field ϕ \phi , multiple delicate features and miraculous monotonic properties of the Einstein-(real) scalar field system are not present. We develop a systematical approach to incorporate Q Q and the complex-valued ϕ \phi into the integrated arguments. For instance, we discover a new path, employing the reduced mass ratio, to establish the sharp trapped surface formation criterion for the charged case. Due to the complex structure and the absence of translational symmetry of ϕ \phi , we also carry out detailed modified scale-critical BV area estimates with renormalized quantities to deal with Q Q and ϕ \phi . We present a new C 1 C^{1} extension criterion by utilizing the Doppler exponent to elucidate the blueshift effect, analogous to the role of integrating vorticity in the Beale-Kato-Madja breakdown criterion for incompressible fluids. Furthermore, by utilizing only double-null foliations, we establish the desired first and second instability theorems for the charged scenarios and identify generic initial conditions for the non-appearance of naked singularities. Our instability argument requires intricate generalizations of the treatment for the uncharged case via analyzing the precise contribution of the charged terms and its connection to the reduced mass ratio.



1.
Introduction

In general relativity, the validity of the weak cosmic censorship is a central unresolved issue. It is conjectured that
for generic initial data, the singularities formed in the gravitational collapse would be confined within black-hole regions and cannot be seen by distant observers
. In 1969 Penrose
[
44
]
wrote:
We are thus presented with what is perhaps the most fundamental unanswered question of general-relativistic collapse theory, namely: does there exist a “cosmic censor” who forbids the appearance of naked singularities, clothing each one in an absolute event horizon?
More precisely, as written by Christodoulou
[
22
]
, the weak cosmic censorship conjecture for Einstein’s equations states:

Generic asymptotically flat initial data have a maximal future development possessing a complete future null infinity.

Christodoulou
[
22
]
further explained:
In heuristic terms, this means that, if we disregard exceptional initial conditions, no singularities are observed from infinity, even though observations from infinity are allowed to continue indefinitely.
So far, only for the spherically symmetric Einstein-(real) scalar field system, this censorship was rigorously established and it was proved by Christodoulou in 1990s through a series of breakthrough results
[
18
]
-
[
21
]
. To generalize this celebrated conclusion to the more physical scenario, one would consider adding the joint evolution of the electric charge
Q
, often viewed as the poor man’s angular momentum, to the Einstein field equations. This motivates the study of the spherically symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field system. However, proving the weak cosmic censorship for this system remains open for more than 20 years.

Returning to Christodoulou’s proof in
[
18
]
-
[
21
]
, integrated arguments with five indispensable steps are established. In each of these steps, he explored the delicate structures of the Einstein-(real) scalar field system and established sharp results. Based on these, he designed a spectacular blueprint for the proof. All these steps and the corresponding sharp results are later ingeniously incorporated into one piece, hence proving the final conclusion. In his proof, various monotonic formulas tied to the special structures of the Einstein-(real) scalar field system are critically employed. Taking the evolution of
Q
into account, the subtle structure changes. The given monotonic formulas for the uncharged case are no longer present, and numerous crucial arguments in
[
18
]
-
[
21
]
have to be circumvented or modified.

In this article, we prove that,
within spherical symmetry, the weak cosmic censorship holds for the gravitational collapse of the Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field system.
Here we consider the
3+1
dimensional spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g,\phi,F)
with
\mathcal{M}
being the
3+1
dimensional manifold,
g
being the Lorenztian metric,
\phi
being the complex-valued function on
\mathcal{M}
and
F
being a differential
2
-form on
\mathcal{M}
. The system of equations governing these quantities takes the form:

(1.1)

\begin{split}R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R&=8\pi T_{\mu\nu}^{SF}+8\pi T_{\mu\nu}^{EM},\\
T_{\mu\nu}^{SF}&=\frac{1}{2}(D_{\mu}\phi)(D_{\nu}\phi)^{\dagger}+\frac{1}{2}(D_{\nu}\phi)(D_{\mu}\phi)^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}D_{\alpha}\phi(D_{\beta}\phi)^{\dagger},\\
T_{\mu\nu}^{EM}&=\frac{1}{4\pi}(g^{\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\mu}F_{\beta\nu}-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}F^{\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta})\\
\end{split}

with
†
standing for the complex conjugate and
D_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}+\mathfrak{e}iA_{\mu}
being the gauge covariant derivative, where
\mathfrak{e}
is the coupling constant, and
A_{\mu}
is the electromagnetic potential. Note that, via the second Bianchi identity, the divergence-free property of the Einstein tensor also implies an evolution equation for the electromagnetic field, i.e.,

(1.2)

\nabla^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}=2\pi\mathfrak{e}i\big(\phi(D_{\mu}\phi)^{\dagger}-\phi^{\dagger}(D_{\mu}\phi)\big).

1.1.
Main Theorem

In this study, we conduct all the proofs with double null coordinates. Under spherical symmetry, the spacetime metric
g
now takes the form:

(1.3)

g=-\Omega^{2}(u,v)\;\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v+r^{2}(u,v)\;(\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi^{2})

with
\Omega(u,v)
called the lapse function and
r(u,v)
called the radial function. Here we employ
u
and
v
as coordinates and they are optical functions toward the future satisfying
g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}u\partial_{\nu}u=0
and
g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}v\partial_{\nu}v=0
. Furthermore, constant
u
and constant
v
hypersurfaces correspond to the outgoing and incoming null cones, respectively.

Our main conclusion is as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

(rough version) In the setting of gravitational collapse for the spherically symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field system, for generic
1
1

1

Here the term “generic” means that the subset of initial conditions leading to the formation of naked singularities has positive co-dimensions.
asymptotically flat initial data prescribed along
C^{+}_{0}
, the singularities formed in the evolution would be covered by a black-hole region.

\mathcal{O}
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
C_{0}^{+}
\Gamma
\mathcal{EH}
\mathcal{I}^{+}
\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}
\mathcal{B}_{0}
\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{BH}
Legend:
\mathcal{B}_{0}

\mathcal{A}

Figure 1.
In above picture,
\Gamma
corresponds to the set of fixed points under the group action
SO(3)
;
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
stands for the first singularity formed along
\Gamma
in the evolution;
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
and the singular boundary
\mathcal{B}
are censored by the black-hole region (
\mathcal{BH}
);
\mathcal{A}
is the apparent horizon;
\mathcal{EH}
is the event horizon;
\mathcal{I}^{+}
is the future null infinity.

Remark 1
.

The precise version of Theorem
1.1
combines our statements in Theorem
4.1
, Theorem
5.1
, Theorem
6.1
, Theorem
7.1
, Theorem
8.1
and Theorem
9.1
.
The conclusion in Theorem
1.1
also holds for generic asymptotically flat Cauchy initial data. For the convenience of observing the instability mechanism, we state our main theorem with characteristic initial data.

Our proofs in this paper are divided into six main steps:

(1)

Find Novel Estimates for
Q
and
\phi
.
In Section
3
, we derive new estimates for
\phi
and
Q
and develop a novel strategy crucially employed throughout the paper to control related charged terms.

(2)

Obtain the Sharp Criterion for Formation of Trapped Surfaces via a New Approach
.
In Section
4
, via a novel approach, we prove the desired trapped-surface-formation criterion for the charged scenario and state it as Theorem
4.1
. Specifically, restricted to the
Q=0
case, in Appendix
A
, we give a 2-page new proof of Christodoulou’s sharp criterion for the Einstein-(real) scalar field system.

(3)

Establish Scale-Critical
BV
Area Estimates with Renormalizations
. In this step, the objective is to establish technical BV area estimates that are crucial for the entire regularity argument. Section
5
is dedicated to proving Theorem
5.1
for the charged case. Incorporating the evolution of
Q(u,v)
into the system breaks the transitional symmetry of
\phi(u,v)
and complicates the system. These require us to introduce new renormalizations and to conduct heavy intricate calculations. In the process, the complex structure of the system is carefully explored and utilized.

(4)

Present a New
C^{1}
Extension Principle related to the Blueshift Effect
. To connect with the instability arguments, we propose and certify a new
C^{1}
extension principle (Theorem
6.1
) based on the Doppler exponent
\gamma
, which resembles the Beale-Kato-Majda type breakdown criterion, requiring the boundness of the integration of a certain physical quantity. The proof of an equivalent
C^{1}
extension principle for the uncharged scalar field is also listed as Appendix
B
.

(5)

Prove the First Instability Theorem with Double Null Foliations
. In Section
7
, we prove and summarize the first-instability conclusions as Theorem
7.1
with double null foliations. Christodoulou employed Bondi formalism in his original arguments. Our proofs for both the first and second instability theorems inject new ingredients about the charge and associated estimates that are central and delicate to the overall argument.

(6)

Vindicate the Second Instability Theorem with Double Null Foliations
. In Section
8
, we further confirm the second instability mechanism and prove Theorem
8.1
. All the aforementioned components are interconnected, constituting an integrated argument. These eventually lead to Theorem
9.1
in Section
9
describing conclusions of genericness.

To prove the weak cosmic censorship conjecture for the spherically symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field system, we incorporate these steps with the following coherent progression of logical arguments:

-

Near the center
\Gamma
portrayed below, we start to consider the characteristic initial value problem with initial data prescribed along
C_{0}^{+}
. Applying Theorem
5.1
, we extend the BV existence region toward the future, with scale-critical BV area estimates controlled.

-

Assume that
O^{\prime}
is the first singular point along
\Gamma
. For any regular point
R
along
\Gamma
before
O^{\prime}
, we consider a triangular shadowed region with the future tip at
R
. Within this region, a blueshift factor
e^{\gamma}
(referred to as the Doppler factor, where
\gamma
is termed the Doppler exponent) is bounded. Physically, the Doppler factor
e^{\gamma}
measures the blue shift of a photon emitted backward from any
(u,v)
in the shadowed region and measured by an observer located at
(0,v)
along
C_{0}^{+}
. As
R
approaches
O^{\prime}
along
\Gamma
, by employing Theorem
6.1
, along the backward cone
v=v_{0}
emitting from
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, we have
\gamma\rightarrow+\infty
as it approaches
O^{\prime}
.

-

The fact that
\gamma\rightarrow+\infty
along
v=v_{0}
triggers the blueshift-driven instability mechanism. We then employ the first and the second instability theorems (together with the trapped surface formation criterion) in the region between
v=v_{0}
and
v=v_{1}
to arrive at the main conclusion.

1.2.
Equations

Before we explain our proof strategy, we review several physical quantities and state their evolution equations below. The
Hawking mass

m(u,v)
for sphere
S(u,v)
on
\mathcal{Q}
is defined via

(1.4)

1-\frac{2m}{r}=g(\nabla r,\nabla r)=-4\Omega^{-2}\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r.

The
mass ratio

\mu(u,v)
for sphere
S(u,v)
is further set to be

(1.5)

\mu(u,v):=\frac{2m(u,v)}{r(u,v)}.

Given the electromagnetic
2
-form
F(u,v)
in (
1.1
), we obtain the charge
Q(u,v)
contained in a sphere
S(u,v)
implicitly by solving

(1.6)

F(u,v)=F_{uv}\;\mathrm{d}u\wedge\mathrm{d}v=\frac{Q(u,v)}{2r^{2}(u,v)}\Omega^{2}(u,v)\;\mathrm{d}u\wedge\mathrm{d}v.

Equivalently, an explicit expression for
Q(u,v)
is given by

(1.7)

Q(u,v):=2r^{2}\Omega^{-2}F_{uv}(u,v).

At the same time, notice that each electromagnetic field
F_{uv}
is associated with a
1
-form
A_{u}
, called the electromagnetic potential. Furthermore,
F_{uv}
can be rewritten as

(1.8)

F_{uv}=\partial_{u}A_{v}-\partial_{v}A_{u}.

As explained in
[
35
]
, when
\mathfrak{e}\neq 0
, the system (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) is invariant under the following local
U(1)
gauge transformations:

(1.9)

\displaystyle\phi\rightarrow e^{-\mathfrak{e}\mathrm{i}f}\phi,\quad A_{\mu}\rightarrow A_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}f

with
f
being a smooth real-valued function to be fixed. In this study, we impose the gauge condition

A_{v}=0

by choosing the appropriate
f
. With this gauge choice, we reduce (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) to

(1.10)

\displaystyle F_{uv}=-\partial_{v}A_{u}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad Q(u,v)=-2r^{2}\Omega^{-2}\partial_{v}A_{u}.

Substituting (
2.1
) and (
1.10
) back to the field equations (
1.1
) and (
1.2
), we then derive the following evolution system:

(1.11)

r\partial_{v}\partial_{u}r+\partial_{v}r\partial_{u}r=-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4}\left(1-\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\right),

(1.12)

r^{2}\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log(\Omega)=-2\pi r^{2}(D_{u}\phi(\partial_{v}\phi)^{\dagger}+\partial_{v}\phi(D_{u}\phi)^{\dagger})-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{2}\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{2}+\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r,

(1.13)

\partial_{u}(\Omega^{-2}\partial_{u}r)=-4\pi r\Omega^{-2}|D_{u}\phi|^{2},

(1.14)

\partial_{v}(\Omega^{-2}\partial_{v}r)=-4\pi r\Omega^{-2}|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2},

(1.15)

r\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\phi+\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}\phi+\partial_{v}r\partial_{u}\phi+\mathfrak{e}\mathrm{i}\Psi(A)=0,

(1.16)

\Psi(A)=A_{u}\partial_{v}(r\phi)-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4}\frac{Q}{r}\phi,

(1.17)

\partial_{u}Q=4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}\im{(\phi^{\dagger}D_{u}\phi)},

(1.18)

\partial_{v}Q=-4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}\im{(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)}.

Note that in the equations above we use the notation
D_{u}:=\partial_{u}+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}
for the covariant derivative. Moreover,the coupling constant
\mathfrak{e}
here satisfies
0\leq\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}
. We also emphasize that, in the above system,
\phi
is a complex-valued unknown function, while
r,A_{u},\Omega^{2},\im(\phi)
,
\re(\phi)
are real-valued unknown functions.

In our arguments, derived from (
1.11
), (
1.13
), (
1.14
), (
1.16
), we also frequently employ the following equations:

(1.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\partial_{u}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r,

(1.20)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}(1-\mu)}{(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)},

(1.21)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\frac{1-\mu}{-\partial_{u}r}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi r|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}(1-\mu)}{(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)},

(1.22)

\displaystyle\Psi(A)

\displaystyle=A_{u}\partial_{v}(r\phi)+\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}{r(1-\mu)}.

For the wave equation in (
1.15
), by (
1.16
), we also obtain and utilize the following equivalent forms:

(1.23)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(r\partial_{v}\phi\right)+(\partial_{v}r)(\partial_{u}\phi)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}\partial_{v}(r\phi)-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}{r(1-\mu)},

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(r\partial_{u}\phi\right)+(\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}\phi)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}\partial_{v}(r\phi)-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}{r(1-\mu)},

and

(1.24)

\displaystyle D_{u}(r\partial_{v}\phi)+\partial_{v}r(D_{u}\phi)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}{r(1-\mu)},

\displaystyle\partial_{v}(rD_{u}\phi)+\partial_{u}r(\partial_{v}\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}{r(1-\mu)}.

It is also instructive to compute the evolution equations for
m
and for
\mu
. These equations are

(1.25)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}m

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{(-\partial_{u}r)}-\frac{Q^{2}(-\partial_{u}r)}{2r^{2}},

(1.26)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}m

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}+\frac{Q^{2}\partial_{v}r}{2r^{2}},

(1.27)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\mu

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi r(1-\mu)|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{(-\partial_{u}r)}+\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\left(\mu-\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\right),

(1.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\mu

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi r(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}-\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}\left(\mu-\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\right).

In addition, the terms
\partial_{v}(Q^{2}/r^{2})
,
\partial_{u}(Q^{2}/r^{2})
,
\partial_{v}A_{u}
appear fairly often in later sections. We can obtain their expressions via using (
1.17
), (
1.18
), and (
1.10
) as follows:

(1.29)

\partial_{v}\left(\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)=-8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)-\frac{2Q^{2}\partial_{v}r}{r^{3}},

(1.30)

\partial_{u}\left(\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)=8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q\im(\phi^{\dagger}D_{u}\phi)-\frac{2Q^{2}\partial_{u}r}{r^{3}},

(1.31)

\partial_{v}A_{u}=\frac{2Q\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{r^{2}(1-\mu)}.

A physical quantity of great interest in this paper is the
Doppler exponent
. For any
(u,v),(\overline{u},v)\in\mathcal{Q}
, we define
\gamma(u,v;\overline{u})
to be

(1.32)

\gamma(u,v;\overline{u}):=\int_{\overline{u}}^{u}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

In particular, if we consider initial data prescribed along the
u=0
hypersurface, we denote
\gamma(u,v)
to be the associated
Doppler exponent
at
(u,v)
, given by

(1.33)

\gamma(u,v):=\int_{0}^{u}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

For the uncharged case, in
[
21
,
22
]
Christodoulou employed this quantity to study the instability mechanism. In this paper, in addition to the instability theorems, we also use
\gamma(u,v)
to establish the
C^{1}
extension theorem. Note that the etymology of this term in this paper stems from the following
Doppler identity
derived from (
1.19
):

(1.34)

(\partial_{v}r)(0,v)=(\partial_{v}r)(u,v)\exp\left(\int^{u}_{0}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right)=(\partial_{v}r)(u,v)e^{\gamma(u,v)}.

Furthermore, we will refer to
e^{\gamma(u,v)}
as the Doppler factor, and
\gamma(u,v)
as the associated Doppler exponent. Physically, we can view the quantity
\partial_{v}r(u,v)
as a proxy for the local scale factor, which measures the wavelength of a photon emitted (to the past) from
(u,v)
. The Doppler identity in (
1.34
) implies that an observer at
(0,v)
would observe the same photon with its wavelength scaled by a factor of
e^{\gamma(u,v)}
. On the other hand, if a photon is emitted from
(0,v)
towards
(u,v)
, an observer at
(u,v)
would observe the same photon with its wavelength scaled by a factor of
e^{-\gamma(u,v)}
. In both cases, if
\gamma(u,v)>0
, the former corresponds to the
redshift effect
, while the latter corresponds to the
blueshift effect
. The
Doppler factor
is thus a proxy for the extent in which the wavelength of a photon is being redshifted or blueshifted. A parallel argument using the notion of proper time and length to justify the redshift and blueshift effects is given in
[
28
]
.

It is worth noting that along
\Gamma
the Doppler exponent
\gamma(v,v)
defined in (
1.33
) is the integral of a dimensionless quantity. As we will see in Theorem
6.1
, the breakdown of regularity is signaled by the divergence of
\gamma(v,v)
. This is consistent with Christodoulou’s comment in
[
22
]
on finding an analogous quantity to the integration of vorticity in the breakdown criterion for the incompressible 3D Euler equation as in
[
13
]
.

We can further compute the derivatives of
\gamma
with respect to
u
and
v
as follows. For the former, we have

(1.35)

\partial_{u}\gamma(u,v)=\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u,v)

if
\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u,v)
is an integrable function in its corresponding domain. For the latter, we first calculate

(1.36)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)+\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)\partial_{v}\left(\frac{1-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}-1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}r)r+(\partial_{v}r)(\partial_{u}r)}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)+\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)\left(\frac{-\partial_{v}(Q^{2}/r^{2})}{1-\mu}+\frac{1-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{(1-\mu)^{2}}\partial_{v}\mu\right).

With (
1.19
), we have

(1.37)

\frac{(-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}r)r+(\partial_{v}r)(\partial_{u}r)}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)=\frac{(\partial_{v}r)(\partial_{u}r)}{r^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)\left(1-\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)\right).

Employing (
1.28
) and (
1.29
), we also rewrite

(1.38)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)\left(\frac{-\partial_{v}(Q^{2}/r^{2})}{1-\mu}+\frac{1-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{(1-\mu)^{2}}\partial_{v}\mu\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)\left(\frac{8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)}{1-\mu}+\frac{2Q^{2}\partial_{v}r}{r^{3}(1-\mu)}+\frac{1-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{(1-\mu)^{2}}\left(\frac{4\pi r(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}+\frac{-\partial_{v}r}{r}\left(\mu-\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{r^{2}}\right)\left(-\frac{2Q^{2}}{r^{2}(1-\mu)}+\frac{(\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2})(1-Q^{2}/r^{2})}{(1-\mu)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)\left(\frac{8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)}{1-\mu}\right)+\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)\left(\frac{4\pi r(1-Q^{2}/r^{2})|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r(1-\mu)}\right).

Combining (
1.37
) and (
1.38
) back to (
1.36
), we hence obtain

(1.39)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}

with

(1.40)

T_{1}:=\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\left(\frac{8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)}{1-\mu}\right),\;T_{2}:=\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\left(\frac{4\pi r(1-Q^{2}/r^{2})|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r(1-\mu)}\right),\;T_{3}:=\frac{2\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\mu-2Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right).

Back to (
1.33
), this implies

(1.41)

\partial_{v}\gamma(u,v)=\int_{0}^{u}(T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3})(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

if we can justify the interchange between the derivative and the integral.

1.3.
Key Elements and New Ingredients

In this subsection, we outline some central ideas and remark on several new ingredients for each of the important steps.

1.3.1.
Novel Estimates for
Q
and
\phi

In Section
3
, we will show that
|Q|
and
|Q\phi|
enjoy enhanced estimates, which are heavily used throughout this paper. To explain this, we start by listing the estimate for
\phi
:

(1.42)

\begin{split}|\phi|(u,v)\leq&\;|\phi|(\bar{u},v)+\int_{\bar{u}}^{u}|D_{u}\phi|(u^{\prime},v)du^{\prime}\\
\leq&\;|\phi|(\bar{u},v)+\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}}\bigg(\int_{\bar{{u}}}^{u}\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{-\partial_{u}r}(u^{\prime},v)du^{\prime}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\int_{\bar{u}}^{u}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}(u^{\prime},v)du^{\prime}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
\leq&\;|\phi|(\bar{u},v)+\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v)}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\bar{u},v)}\right)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\log\left(\frac{r(\bar{u},v)}{r(u,v)}\right)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

Here, data along
u=\bar{u}
are considered given. The first inequality in (
1.42
) utilizes Grönwall’s inequality while the second inequality employs

\partial_{u}\log\left(\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}\right)=\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{-\partial_{u}r}.

Note that the above identity also implies
\partial_{u}(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu})\leq 0
, and this could give an upper bound for
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}
. However, it fails to offer an upper bound for
\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}
and
\log\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v)}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\bar{u},v)}
. Hence, potentially utilizing (
1.42
) to control
\phi
and related terms could lead to non-uniform bounds.

In this paper, we overcome this problem and similar difficulties by spotting and employing a new strategy. Take the estimate for
|Q|
as an example. We first consider the expression for
\int_{u}^{v}\partial_{v}Q(u,v^{\prime})dv^{\prime}
. Applying the above estimate (
1.42
) for
\phi
and the Hölder’s inequality, as shown in (
3.15
)-(
3.20
), for each give
\chi\in(0,1)
, we obtain

\displaystyle|Q|

\displaystyle(u,v)\leq\int^{v}_{u}4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\pi\mathfrak{e}\int^{v}_{u}\frac{r^{2}(u,v^{\prime})}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}+l.o.t.

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(8\pi^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{r^{2}\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+l.o.t.

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{m^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)}{(8\pi^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\int^{v}_{u}r^{2}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot\left(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+l.o.t.

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}(\bar{u},v;\chi)\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)r^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)\cdot\left(\int^{v}_{u}r^{2-\chi}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot r^{\chi}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})\cdot\left(\frac{r^{\chi}(u,v^{\prime})}{r^{\chi}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+l.o.t.

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}(\bar{u},v;\chi)\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{\chi}{2}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-u)^{\frac{1}{2}}

with constant
C_{1}(\bar{u},v;\chi)
provided in (
3.20
). Here, to get rid of the dangerous
\bigg(\log\bigg(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v)}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\bar{u},v)}\bigg)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}
term in the second line, we crucially utilize the fact that

after applying the Hölder’s inequality for
|\partial_{v}\phi|
, there is an additional
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}
introduced in the expression (as in the third inequality).
By viewing
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}
as
x
, we then employ the inequality that, for any
x\in(0,1)
and
c>0
, we have

x^{c}\log\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\leq\frac{1}{ce}.

Hence, a uniform upper bound is obtained, and the worrisome term in the logarithm is thus controlled. Note that this strategy is employed throughout this paper. With this strengthened bound for
|Q|
, we arrive at yet another frequently used estimate, as follows:

(1.43)

\bigg(\mu-\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\bigg)(u,v)\geq\mu(u,v)\left(1-C_{1}^{2}(\bar{u},v;\chi)r^{1-\chi}(u,v)r^{\chi}(\bar{u},v)(v-u)\right)>0.

Besides
|Q|
, we also derive an improved estimate for
|Q\phi|
. This is done by utilizing
\partial_{v}Q=-4\pi er^{2}\mbox{Im}(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)
together with (
3.21
)-(
3.39
) to obtain

\displaystyle|Q\phi|^{2}(u,v)\leq

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}2|Q\phi|\partial_{v}(Q\phi)(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}2(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{2}|Q|+|\phi||Q|^{2})|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)}(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{3}|Q|+|\phi||Q|^{2})^{2}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)}(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{3}|Q|+|\phi||Q|^{2})^{2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}m(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mu(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}r^{2-\frac{\chi}{2}}(u,v).

For the final inequality, we use (
1.42
) to control
|\phi|
and apply our new strategy with the gained factor
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}
. Note that
|Q\phi|
obeys a better bound than the product of individual bounds for
|Q|
and
|\phi|
.

1.3.2.
Trapped Surface Formation Criterion

In this paper, we take a new approach to establish the desired trapped surface formation criterion for the charged case. With these new arguments, in the appendix, we also give a direct proof for the uncharged case to retrieve Christodoulou’s sharp criterion in
[
18
]
. Let us begin by first explaining some key calculations in
[
18
]
. With the mass ratio
\eta=\frac{m_{2}-m_{1}}{r_{2}}
introduced by Christodoulou, for the uncharged case, we can compute the following:

(1.44)

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\mathrm{d}u}}{\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}u}}=-\frac{\eta}{x}-\frac{16\pi\partial_{v}r_{2}\Omega_{2}^{-2}}{x\partial_{u}r_{2}}\bigg(r_{2}^{2}|\partial_{u}\phi_{2}|^{2}-\frac{\Omega_{1}^{-2}\partial_{v}r_{1}}{\Omega_{2}^{-2}\partial_{v}r_{2}}r_{1}^{2}|\partial_{u}\phi_{1}|^{2}\bigg).\end{split}

Based on the particular structures of the Einstein-scalar field system, via using
\Theta:=r_{2}\partial_{u}\phi_{2}-r_{1}\partial_{u}\phi_{1}
and
several ingenious lemmas, one could further prove

(1.45)

\begin{split}r_{2}^{2}|\partial_{u}\phi_{2}|^{2}-\frac{\Omega_{1}^{-2}\partial_{v}r_{1}}{\Omega_{2}^{-2}\partial_{v}r_{2}}r_{1}^{2}|\partial_{u}\phi_{1}|^{2}\leq&\;r_{2}^{2}|\partial_{u}\phi_{2}|^{2}-e^{\eta}r_{1}^{2}|\partial_{u}\phi_{1}|^{2}\\
\leq&\;\Theta^{2}+2\Theta\partial_{u}\phi_{1}r_{1}+(1-e^{\eta})r_{1}^{2}|\partial_{u}\phi_{1}|^{2}\\
\leq&\;\bigg(1+\frac{1}{e^{\eta}-1}\bigg)\Theta^{2}\leq\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\eta}\bigg)\Theta^{2}\\
\leq&\;\bigg(1+\frac{1}{e^{\eta}-1}\bigg)\cdot\frac{\partial_{u}r_{2}}{8\pi\Omega_{2}^{-2}\partial_{v}r_{2}}(m_{2}-m_{1})\bigg(\frac{1}{r_{2}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}\bigg).\end{split}

Together with (
1.44
), this gives

(1.46)

\begin{split}\frac{d\eta}{dx}\leq&-\frac{\eta}{x}-\frac{2}{x}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\eta}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{1}{r_{2}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}\bigg)(m_{2}-m_{1})\\
=&-\frac{\eta}{x}+\frac{\eta}{x}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\eta}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}-1\bigg).\end{split}

Solving this ODE inequality then leads to a desired criterion for the formation of trapped surfaces. The first author and Lim tried to generalize this approach in
[
18
]
to the charged scenario. However, for the charged case, a few inequalities in (
1.45
) cannot be as sharp as for the uncharged case. Hence, the result obtained by An-Lim in
[
6
]
is slightly weaker than the desired one, making it incompatible with the instability arguments we seek to employ.

We then state our new approach in this paper as follows. We begin by demonstrating it for the uncharged case.
2
2

2

The complete proof for the uncharged case is in the appendix.

With the mass ratio
\eta
, we compute and rewrite the expression of its derivative as

(1.47)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\mathrm{d}u}}{\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}u}}=\frac{\partial_{u}\left(\frac{2(m_{2}-m_{1})}{r_{2}}\right)}{\frac{\partial_{u}r_{2}}{r_{2}(u_{0})}}=-\frac{\eta}{x}-\frac{2}{x(\partial_{u}r_{2})}\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}m\;\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Employing
\partial_{v}m=2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)(\partial_{v}\phi)^{2}/\partial_{v}r
, we further calculate

\displaystyle-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}m

\displaystyle=-\partial_{u}\left(\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)(\partial_{v}\phi)^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\left(\partial_{u}\left(\frac{(1-\mu)}{\partial_{v}r}\right)r^{2}(\partial_{v}\phi)^{2}+\left(\frac{2(1-\mu)}{\partial_{v}r}\right)r(\partial_{v}\phi)\partial_{u}(r\partial_{v}\phi)\right)

\displaystyle=8\pi^{2}r^{3}(1-\mu)\left((\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)\frac{(\partial_{u}\phi)^{2}(\partial_{v}\phi)^{2}}{(\partial_{u}r)^{2}(\partial_{v}r)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\pi r^{2}}(\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)\frac{(\partial_{v}\phi)(\partial_{u}\phi)}{(\partial_{v}r)(\partial_{u}r)}\right).

Utilizing the elementary inequality
-x^{2}+ax\leq{a^{2}}/{4}
for
x,a\in\mathbb{R}
, we immediately obtain

\displaystyle\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}m\;\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}8\pi^{2}r^{3}(1-\mu)(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)\left(-\frac{(\partial_{u}\phi)^{2}(\partial_{v}\phi)^{2}}{(\partial_{u}r)^{2}(\partial_{v}r)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\pi r^{2}}\frac{(\partial_{v}\phi)(\partial_{u}\phi)}{(\partial_{v}r)(\partial_{u}r)}\right)\;\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}8\pi^{2}r^{3}(1-\mu)(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)\left(\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}r^{4}}\right)\;\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)_{2}}{2}\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}=\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)_{2}}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{r_{2}(u)-r_{1}(u)}{r_{1}(u)}\right)\leq\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)_{2}}{2}\delta(u).

Here, we define
\delta(u):=(r_{2}(u)-r_{1}(u))/r_{1}(u)
, and we further denote
\delta_{0}
to be the corresponding initial value.

Back to (
1.47
), this leads to an ODE inequality of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\mathrm{d}x}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\eta}{x}+\frac{\delta(u)}{x}\leq-\frac{\eta}{x}+\frac{\delta_{0}}{x(x(1+\delta_{0})-\delta_{0})}.

Upon solving the ODE inequality above, we then get the desired result. In this new approach, we tend to use identities instead of inequalities, which relies on fewer requirements of the structures of equations.

We now generalize this new approach to the charged scenario. We first define a new quantity

\eta^{*}(u):=\frac{2(m(u,v_{2})-m(u,v_{1}))}{r(u,v_{2})}-\frac{2}{r(u,v_{2})}\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}\frac{Q^{2}(\partial_{v}r)}{2r^{2}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Note that outside the trapped region, this quantity is less or equal to the standard mass ratio, and we refer to it as the
reduced mass ratio
, denoted by
\eta^{*}(u)
. We conclude that, for the charged case, if it satisfies the condition:

(1.48)

\eta^{*}(u_{0})>C_{24}\frac{\delta(u_{0})}{1+\delta(u_{0})}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u_{0})}\right)+C_{23}\left(\frac{\delta(u_{0})r_{1}(u_{0})}{1-\mu^{*}(u_{0})}\right)

with the constants
C_{23}
,
C_{24}
given in (
4.35
), (
4.40
), and
\mu^{*}(u_{0})
being the supremum of
\mu(u,v)
along
u=u_{0}
, then a MOTS or a trapped surface is guaranteed to form in the evolution. We emphasize that this is a crucial criterion. The factor
\frac{1}{1-\mu^{*}(u_{0})}
is related to the blueshift effect, and the additional
r_{1}(u_{0})
term will play a pivotal role in establishing the corresponding instability theorems.

For the proof of this conclusion, we proceed as in (
1.47
) and obtain

(1.49)

\begin{split}\frac{d\eta^{*}}{dx}=\frac{\frac{d\eta^{*}}{du}}{\frac{dx}{du}}=-\frac{\eta^{*}}{x}-\frac{2}{x(\partial_{u}r_{2})}\bigg(\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}[-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}m+\partial_{u}\left(\frac{Q^{2}\partial_{v}r}{2r^{2}}\right)]\bigg).\end{split}

Applying the equations for
m
and for
Q
, we get

(1.50)

\displaystyle-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}m+\partial_{u}\left(\frac{Q^{2}\partial_{v}r}{2r^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial_{v}r)(-\partial_{u}r)\left(-8\pi^{2}r^{3}(1-\mu)\left(\frac{|D_{u}\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|}{(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}\right)^{2}-4\pi r(1-\mu)\frac{\re{((\partial_{v}\phi)^{\dagger}(D_{u}\phi))}}{\partial_{v}r(-\partial_{u}r)}\right)

\displaystyle+4\pi\mathfrak{e}Q(-\partial_{u}r)\im{(\phi^{\dagger}(\partial_{v}\phi))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8\pi^{2}r^{3}(\partial_{v}r)(-\partial_{u}r)(1-\mu)\left(-\left(\frac{|D_{u}\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|}{(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2\pi r^{2}}\frac{|D_{u}\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|}{(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}\right)+4\pi\mathfrak{e}|Q|(-\partial_{u}r)|\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|.

Back to (
1.49
), we then integrate the first two terms on the right of (
1.50
) and obtain

\displaystyle\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}8\pi^{2}r^{3}(\partial_{v}r)(1-\mu)\left(-\left(\frac{|D_{u}\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|}{(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2\pi r^{2}}\frac{|D_{u}\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|}{(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}\right)(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}2\pi^{2}r^{3}(\partial_{v}r)(1-\mu)\left(\frac{1}{2\pi r^{2}}\right)^{2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{r(u,v_{2})}{r(u,v_{1})}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{r(u,v_{2})-r(u,v_{1})}{r(u,v_{1})}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\delta(u).

To bound the integration of the last term in (
1.50
), by applying the obtained estimates for
|Q\phi|
, with
\chi\in\left(0,\frac{1}{3}\right]
we derive

\displaystyle\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}4\pi\mathfrak{e}|Q||\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{4\pi}\mathfrak{e}{\eta^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}r_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}(u)\left(\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}\frac{|Q|^{2}|\phi|^{2}}{r^{2}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{4\pi}\mathfrak{e}C_{5}{\eta^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}r_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}(u)\left(\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u_{0},v^{\prime})r^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3\chi}{2}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{4\pi}\mathfrak{e}C_{5}}{(1-\mu_{0}^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\eta^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}r_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}-\frac{3\chi}{4}}(u)r_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}(u)\delta(u_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

These together with
(-\partial_{u}r)(u,v^{\prime})\leq(-\partial_{u}r)_{2}(u)
for
v^{\prime}\in[v_{1},v_{2}]
give

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta^{*}}{\mathrm{d}x}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\eta^{*}}{x}-\frac{2}{x(\partial_{u}r_{2})}\left(\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}m+\partial_{u}\left(\frac{Q^{2}\partial_{v}r}{2r^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\eta^{*}}{x}+\frac{\delta}{x}+\frac{1}{x}\frac{4\sqrt{\pi}\mathfrak{e}C_{5}}{(1-\mu_{0}^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\eta^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}r_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}-\frac{3\chi}{4}}(u)r_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}(u_{0})\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\eta^{*}}{x}+\frac{\delta}{x}+C_{21}x^{-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{3\chi}{4}}\left(\frac{2{\eta^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(r_{1})_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(1-\mu_{0}^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\eta^{*}}{x}+\frac{\delta}{x}+\frac{C_{21}}{x^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\eta^{*}+\frac{\delta_{0}\cdot(r_{1})_{0}}{1-\mu_{0}^{*}}\right)

with
C_{21}=2\sqrt{\pi}\mathfrak{e}C_{5}r(\overline{u},v_{3})^{\frac{3}{4}-\frac{3\chi}{4}}
. Solving this ODE then leads us to the desired trapped surface formation criterion for the charged case.

1.3.3.
BV Area Estimates

In Section
5
, we establish the scale-critical BV area estimates in a region
\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})
with
v_{0}
small. We start by recalling the definition for the area of a pair of functions and it is given by

A[f,g]=\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial u}\frac{\partial g}{\partial v}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial v}\frac{\partial g}{\partial u}\right|\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

Notice that, unlike the non-charged case, our system (
1.11
) - (
1.18
) is
not
invariant under the transformation
r\mapsto r
,
\Omega\mapsto\Omega
,
\phi\mapsto\phi+c
.
3
3

3

This is translation transformation for
\phi
.
We can no longer assume
\phi(0,0)=0
without loss of generality as in
[
19
]
for the uncharged case. Henceforth, in this paper, we conduct renormalizations for terms involving
\phi
and meticulously define the following quantities:

\displaystyle\phi_{0}:=\phi(0,0),\quad\tilde{\phi}:=\phi-\phi_{0},\quad\lambda:=\partial_{v}r,\quad\nu:=\partial_{u}r,

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}:=r\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi},\quad\quad\quad\tilde{\zeta}:=r\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi},\quad\quad\quad\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\zeta}$}}:=rD_{u}\phi,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}:=\partial_{v}(r\phi),\quad\mathfrak{b}:=\partial_{u}(r\phi),\quad\alpha:=\frac{\mathfrak{a}}{\lambda},\quad\beta:=\frac{\mathfrak{b}}{\nu},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}:=\partial_{v}(r\tilde{\phi}),\quad\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}:=\partial_{u}(r\tilde{\phi}),\quad\tilde{\alpha}:=\frac{\alpha}{\lambda},\quad\tilde{\beta}:=\frac{\beta}{\nu},

\displaystyle A:=\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|,\quad B:=\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}|,\quad P:=\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}|\tilde{\phi}|.

We further set

\displaystyle\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}:=\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}r\phi,\quad X:=\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}\int_{u}^{v_{0}}|\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi}|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime},\quad Y:=\sup_{v\in(0,v_{0})}\int_{v}^{0}|\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi}|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime},

and let

D:=TV_{\{0\}\times(0,v_{0})}[\mathfrak{a}]=TV_{\{0\}\times(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}].

Note that in
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\zeta}$}},\alpha,\beta,\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}
, we exclude the use of
\tilde{\phi}
, and the form
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}
is specifically tailored for the charged scenario. Additionally, notice that the size of the initial data is measured in
D
and is small. Our obtained area estimates are summarized in the theorem below.

Theorem.
(
BV
Area Estimates.)
Assume that

(1.51)

\inf_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\partial_{v}r\geq\frac{5}{12},\quad\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}(-\partial_{u}r)\leq\frac{2}{3},\quad\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\mu\leq\frac{1}{3}

hold for
v_{0}
sufficiently small.
4
4

4

In the regular region, employing a spacetime rescaling as conducted by Christodoulou in Section 6 of
[
19
]
can verify these assumptions.
Then there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
, such that if the initial data satisfy

Z=\max\{X,Y\}\leq\varepsilon_{0}\quad\text{ and }\quad D:=TV_{\{0\}\times(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}]\leq\varepsilon_{0},

then we have
Z\lesssim D
, the total variations satisfy

(1.52)

\begin{aligned} \sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}]&\lesssim D,\\
\sup_{v\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{(0,v)\times\{v\}}[\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}]&\lesssim D,\\
TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|_{\Gamma}]&\lesssim D,\end{aligned}\quad\begin{aligned} \sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\log\lambda]&\lesssim D^{2},\\
\sup_{v\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{(0,v)\times\{v\}}[\log|\nu|]&\lesssim D^{2},\\
TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\log(\lambda|_{\Gamma})]&\lesssim D^{2},\\
\end{aligned}

and the area estimates obey

(1.53)

\begin{aligned} A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}/\lambda]&\lesssim D^{2},\\
A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu]&\lesssim D^{2},\\
\end{aligned}\quad\begin{aligned} A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}]&\lesssim D^{3},\\
A[\nu,\tilde{\phi}]&\lesssim D^{3}.\\
\end{aligned}

Prior to computing the relevant area estimates, we first establish a couple of lemmas in Section
5
which we will described below.

Lemma 1.
For all
(u,v)\in\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0}),
we have

\int^{v_{0}}_{u}|\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi}|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\tilde{\alpha}]\quad\text{ and }\quad\int^{v}_{0}|\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi}|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\leq TV_{(0,v)\times\{v\}}[\tilde{\beta}].

We prove this lemma by utilizing integration by parts and leveraging the fact that the relevant boundary terms vanish along the center
\Gamma
. To proceed, in Section
5
, we then prove

Lemma 2.
Assuming
D\leq 1
,
v_{0}
being sufficiently small and (
1.51
) holds, then we get

A\leq 4D,\quad B\leq 8D,\quad P\leq 12D.

The proof of the lemma is via a bootstrapping argument. We first assume that for a fixed
(\tilde{u},\tilde{v})\in\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0}),

\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|\leq 4D,\quad\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\phi}|\leq 12D,

and attempt to improve on such an estimate. Since
\tilde{\phi}=\frac{1}{r(u,v)}\int^{v}_{u}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}
, we obtain

(1.54)

\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\phi}|\leq\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}\left|\frac{v-u}{r(u,v)}\right|\leq 3\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|.

To control
\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}
, we employ the following equation:

(1.55)

\partial_{u}\left(\partial_{v}\left(r\tilde{\phi}\right)\right)=\tilde{\phi}\partial_{u}\partial_{v}r-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}\partial_{v}(r\tilde{\phi})-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\phi_{0}A_{u}\partial_{v}r-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}\frac{Q\phi}{r}.

By viewing this as a first-order ODE in
\partial_{v}(r\tilde{\phi})
, with the help of an integrating factor, we deduce that

(1.56)

\displaystyle|\partial_{v}(r\tilde{\phi})|(u,v)\leq

\displaystyle|\partial_{v}(r\tilde{\phi})|(0,v)+\int^{u}_{0}|\tilde{\phi}|(-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}r)(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{e}\int^{u}_{0}\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)}{r(1-\mu)}|Q||\phi|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}+\mathfrak{e}|\phi_{0}|\int^{u}_{0}|A_{u}|\partial_{v}r(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

To close the bootstrap, we will use
\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}
to bound
|Q|
and
|A_{u}|
. From (
1.18
), we have

(1.57)

\displaystyle|Q|(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi\mathfrak{e}\int^{v}_{u}r^{2}|\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq 4\pi\mathfrak{e}\left(|\phi_{0}|+\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\phi}|\right)r(u,v)\int^{v}_{u}r|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi\mathfrak{e}\left(|\phi_{0}|+\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\phi}|\right)r(u,v)\int^{v}_{u}|\partial_{v}(r\tilde{\phi})|(u,v^{\prime})+|\tilde{\phi}|(\partial_{v}r)(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi\mathfrak{e}\left(|\phi_{0}|+\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\phi}|\right)r(u,v)\left(\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|\right)(v-u)+\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\phi}|\right)r(u,v)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 24\pi\mathfrak{e}\left(\left|\phi_{0}\right|+\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\phi}|\right)\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|\right)r(u,v)^{2}.

By utilizing (
1.31
) and (
1.57
), we further obtain

(1.58)

\displaystyle|A_{u}|(u^{\prime},v)

\displaystyle\leq\int^{v}_{u^{\prime}}\frac{2|Q|(-\partial_{u}r)\partial_{v}r}{r^{2}(1-\mu)}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq 24\pi v_{0}\mathfrak{e}\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|\right)\left(\left|\phi_{0}\right|+\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,u^{\prime},v)}|\tilde{\phi}|\right).

These two estimates will allow us to deduce that

(1.59)

\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|\right)\leq 3D\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\phi}|\leq 3\left(\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{u},\tilde{v})}|\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|\right)\leq 9D,

which improves the bootstrap assumption in (
1.54
). The desired estimates for
A
and
P
further imply the bound for
B
.

With these preparations, we start the proof of the theorem. Here, we generalize Christodoulou’s ingenious arguments from
[
19
]
to the complex charged scenario. The proof in Section
5
is relies on extensive calculations, and the estimates are intricately connected. Here, we provide an outline. For brevity, given a quantity
H
, we write
H\lesssim D
to convey that there exists a non-negative constant
c
(possibly depending on
\mathfrak{e}
and
|\phi_{0}|
) independent of
D
such that
H\leq cD
. In view of Lemma 2 above, we then have

A\lesssim D,\quad B\lesssim D,\quad P\lesssim D.

Furthermore,
\partial_{v}r|_{\Gamma}=-\partial_{u}r|_{\Gamma}
and (
1.51
) implies

\frac{5}{12}\leq\partial_{v}r(u,v)\leq\frac{1}{2},\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{1}{2}\leq(-\partial_{u}r)(u,v)\leq\frac{2}{3}.

In addition, by

r\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi}=\partial_{v}\left(r\tilde{\phi}\right)-\tilde{\phi}\partial_{v}r,\quad r\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi}=\partial_{u}\left(r\tilde{\phi}\right)-\tilde{\phi}\partial_{u}r,\quad\phi=\phi_{0}+\tilde{\phi},

together with estimates (
1.57
) and (
1.58
),
we deduce that

\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\left|r\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi}\right|\lesssim D,\quad\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\left|r\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi}\right|\lesssim D,\quad\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\left|\phi\right|\lesssim 1,\quad

and

|Q|(u,v)\lesssim Dr^{2}(u,v),\quad|A_{u}|(u,v)\lesssim Dr(u,v),\quad|rD_{u}\phi|(u,v)\lesssim D.

We begin with deriving estimates for
\partial_{v}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}
. This is done by computing
\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}
via using (
5.40
) and we get

(1.60)

\partial_{u}\left(\partial_{v}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}\right)(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})-\partial_{u}\left(\tilde{\phi}\partial_{v}\lambda\right)(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})=G_{1}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})+G_{2}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})+\frac{\partial(\lambda,\tilde{\phi})}{\partial(u,v)}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})

with

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\left(\phi_{0}A_{u}+\frac{\partial_{u}r}{1-\mu}\frac{Q\phi}{r}\right)\partial_{v}\lambda,

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\left((\partial_{v}A_{u})\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}+\phi_{0}(\partial_{v}A_{u})\partial_{v}r+A_{u}\partial_{v}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}+\partial_{v}r\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\partial_{u}r}{1-\mu}\frac{Q\phi}{r}\right)\right).

For each
(u,v)\in\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})
, by integrating with respect to
u^{\prime}
from
0
to
v
and
v^{\prime}
from
u
to
v_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{u}^{v_{0}}\left|\partial_{v}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}-\tilde{\phi}\partial_{v}\lambda\right|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq\int^{v_{0}}_{u}\left|\partial_{v}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}\right|(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}+\int_{u}^{v_{0}}\int^{u}_{0}\left(|G_{1}|+|G_{2}|+\left|\frac{\partial(\lambda,\tilde{\phi})}{\partial(u,v)}\right|\right)(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Taking supremum over
u\in(0,v_{0})
and applying relevant estimates listed at the start of the proof, we derive:

(1.61)

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}]\leq

\displaystyle D+P\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\lambda]+A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}]

\displaystyle+\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}\int_{u}^{v_{0}}\int^{u}_{0}|G_{1}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}+\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}\int_{u}^{v_{0}}\int^{u}_{0}|G_{2}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Furthermore, by applying the obtained estimates, we also show that

(1.62)

\displaystyle\int_{u}^{v_{0}}\int^{u}_{0}|G_{1}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim D\int_{0}^{u}\int^{v_{0}}_{u}|\partial_{v}\lambda|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim D\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\lambda],

and

(1.63)

\int_{u}^{v_{0}}\int^{u}_{0}|G_{2}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}]\lesssim\int_{u}^{v_{0}}\int^{u}_{0}D\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\lesssim D.

These then simplify (
1.61
) to

(1.64)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}]\lesssim

\displaystyle D\left(1+\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\lambda]\right)+A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}].

Moving forward, we proceed to derive estimates for
\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}
. By (
1.24
) and taking derivatives in
u
,
v
, we arrive at

\partial_{v}\partial_{u}\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}=\partial_{v}\left(\tilde{\phi}\partial_{u}\nu\right)+G_{3}+G_{4}-\frac{\partial(\nu,\tilde{\phi})}{\partial(u,v)}

with

\displaystyle G_{3}=\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\partial_{u}\nu\left(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}\frac{Q\phi}{r}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad G_{4}=\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\partial_{u}r\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}\frac{Q\phi}{r}\right).

Integrating along a future-directed outgoing null curve originating from
\Gamma
, we obtain

(1.65)

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{0}\left|\partial_{u}\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}-\tilde{\phi}\partial_{u}\nu\right|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\leq

\displaystyle TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}|_{\Gamma}]+P\cdot TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\nu|_{\Gamma}]+A[\nu,\tilde{\phi}]

\displaystyle+\int^{v}_{0}\int^{v}_{u^{\prime}}|G_{3}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}+\int^{v}_{0}\int^{v}_{u^{\prime}}|G_{4}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

By utilizing the obtained estimates, we deduce that

\int^{v}_{0}\int^{v}_{u^{\prime}}|G_{3}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim D\cdot\sup_{v\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{(0,v)\times\{v\}}[\nu]\quad\text{ and }\quad\int^{v}_{0}\int^{v}_{u^{\prime}}|G_{4}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim D.

Furthermore, by employing relevant regularity conditions along
\Gamma
, we get

TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}|_{\Gamma}]=TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|_{\Gamma}],\quad TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\nu|_{\Gamma}]=TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\lambda|_{\Gamma}].

Combining these with (
1.65
) then yields:

(1.66)

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{(0,v)\times\{v\}}[\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}]\lesssim

\displaystyle TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|_{\Gamma}]+D\left(1+TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\lambda|_{\Gamma}]+\sup_{v\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{(0,v)\times\{v\}}[\nu]\right)+A[\nu,\tilde{\phi}].

On the other hand, if we first integrate along a future-directed incoming null curve
v=u
starting from
C_{0}^{+}
to
(u,u)\in\Gamma
, and then integrate with respect to
u
for
u\in(0,v_{0})
, together with the boundary conditions along
\Gamma
, we will arrive at:

\displaystyle TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|_{\Gamma}]

\displaystyle\leq P\cdot TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\lambda|_{\Gamma}]+D+A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}]+\int_{0}^{v_{0}}\int^{v_{0}}_{u^{\prime}}|G_{1}|(u^{\prime},v)+|G_{2}|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

Using a similar argument for the double integrations of
|G_{1}|
and
|G_{2}|
as in (
1.62
) and (
1.63
), we then deduce that

(1.67)

TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}|_{\Gamma}]\lesssim D\left(1+TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\lambda|_{\Gamma}]+\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\lambda]\right)+A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}].

Next, we will attempt to control
\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\lambda]
appearing in (
1.67
). Starting from (
1.19
), by applying (
1.28
), (
1.29
), and by carefully exploiting the relevant cancellations between the terms, we can show that

(1.68)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\partial_{u}\log\lambda=

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}\right)+\frac{4\pi}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{1-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)\left(\frac{(r|\partial_{v}\phi|)^{2}(\partial_{u}r)}{(\partial_{v}r)}-\frac{(r|D_{u}\phi|)^{2}(\partial_{v}r)}{\partial_{u}r}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q}{1-\mu}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mu}\right)\left((\partial_{u}r)\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)+(\partial_{v}r)\im(\phi^{\dagger}D_{u}\phi)\right)

and

(1.69)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log(-\nu)=

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)-\frac{4\pi}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{1-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)\left(\frac{(r|\partial_{v}\phi|)^{2}(\partial_{u}r)}{(\partial_{v}r)}-\frac{(r|D_{u}\phi|)^{2}(\partial_{v}r)}{\partial_{u}r}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q}{1-\mu}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mu}\right)\left((\partial_{u}r)\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)+(\partial_{v}r)\im(\phi^{\dagger}D_{u}\phi)\right).

Define

\tilde{f}(u,v):=4\pi\re\left(\frac{r(\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi})^{\dagger}}{r}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)\right)(u,v)

and

\tilde{f}^{*}(u,v):=4\pi\re\left(\frac{r(\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi})^{\dagger}}{r}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)\right)(u,v).

By performing laborious computations and utilizing the fact that
(\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi})^{\dagger}(\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi})-(\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi})(\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi})^{\dagger}
is purely imaginary, along with the identity below,

|D_{u}\phi|^{2}=(\partial_{u}\phi+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}\phi)(\partial_{u}\phi-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}\phi)^{\dagger}=|\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi}|^{2}-2\mathfrak{e}A_{u}\im(\phi\partial_{u}\phi^{\dagger})+\mathfrak{e}^{2}A_{u}^{2}|\phi|^{2},

we derive

(1.70)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\partial_{v}\log\lambda\right)=\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}-\tilde{f}\right)+4\pi\re\left(\frac{\partial(\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu)}{\partial(u,v)}\right)+E_{5}+E_{6}

and

(1.71)

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\partial_{u}\log(-\nu)\right)=\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}+\tilde{f}^{*}\right)-4\pi\re\left(\frac{\partial(\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}/\lambda)}{\partial(u,v)}\right)-E_{5}+E_{6}.

Here,
E_{5}
and
E_{6}
are terms satisfying
|E_{5}|\lesssim D^{2}
and
|E_{6}|\lesssim D^{2}
. From (
1.70
), for a fixed
(u,v)\in\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})
, by first integrating along a future-directed incoming null curve and further integrating for
v\in(u,v_{0})
, we obtain

(1.72)

\displaystyle\int^{v_{0}}_{u}\left|\partial_{v}\log\lambda-\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}+\tilde{f}\right|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\lesssim\int^{v_{0}}_{u}\left|\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}-\tilde{f}\right|(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}+A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu]+D^{2}.

By employing relevant derived estimates and boundary conditions along
\Gamma
, we have

\displaystyle\int^{v_{0}}_{u}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{(\partial_{v}r)}{r}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\lesssim DX,

\displaystyle\int^{v_{0}}_{u}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{(\partial_{v}r)}{r}(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\lesssim D^{2},

\displaystyle\int^{v_{0}}_{u}|\tilde{f}|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\lesssim DX,

\displaystyle\int^{v_{0}}_{u}|\tilde{f}|(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\lesssim D^{2}.

Substituting these back to (
1.72
), we arrive at

(1.73)

\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\log\lambda]\lesssim D^{2}+DX+A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu].

An analogous calculation for (
1.71
) yields

(1.74)

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{0}\left|\partial_{u}\log(-\nu)-\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}-4\pi\tilde{f}^{*}\right|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\log(-\nu)|_{\Gamma}]+A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}/\lambda]+D^{2}.

Since

\int^{v}_{0}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim DY+D^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\int^{v}_{0}|\tilde{f}^{*}|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim DY,

inequality (
1.74
) implies

(1.75)

\sup_{v\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{(0,v)\times\{v\}}[\log|\nu|]\lesssim D^{2}+DY+TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\log\lambda|_{\Gamma}]+A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}/\lambda].

By a similar approach for
\log(\lambda|_{\Gamma})
, we also get

(1.76)

TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\log(\lambda|_{\Gamma})]\lesssim D^{2}+A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu].

It now remains to establish relations between the different area terms that have appeared in the estimates above. Starting from
A[\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}/\lambda]
, we observe that

(1.77)

\frac{\partial(\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}/\lambda)}{\partial(u,v)}=\frac{(\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi})^{\dagger}}{\lambda}(\partial_{v}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}-\tilde{\phi}\partial_{v}\lambda)+\frac{(r\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi})}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{\partial(\lambda,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger})}{\partial(u,v)}-\frac{(\partial_{v}\tilde{\phi})^{\dagger}E_{7}}{\lambda}

with
E_{7}
satisfying
|E_{7}|\lesssim Dr
. Since
A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}]=A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}]
and

\int_{D(0,v_{0})}\frac{|\partial_{u}\tilde{\phi}|}{\lambda}|\partial_{v}\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}-\tilde{\phi}\partial_{v}\lambda|(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v\lesssim DY\left(1+\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\lambda]\right)+Y\cdot A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}],

identity (
1.77
) then implies

(1.78)

A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}/\lambda]\lesssim DY\left(1+\sup_{u\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{\{u\}\times(u,v_{0})}[\lambda]\right)+D^{2}+(D+Y)\cdot A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}].

A similar strategy also gives

(1.79)

\displaystyle A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu]\lesssim

\displaystyle X\cdot TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}|_{\Gamma}]+DX\left(1+TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\lambda|_{\Gamma}]+\sup_{v\in(0,v_{0})}TV_{(0,v)\times\{v\}}[\nu]\right)

\displaystyle+D^{2}+(D+X)\cdot A[\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}].

In view of (
1.78
) and (
1.79
), we proceed to estimate
A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}]
and
A[\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}]
. Inspired by the definition of
\tilde{f}
above, we further define

(1.80)

\tilde{g}:=4\pi\im\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}}{r}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)\right)

so that

\tilde{f}+\mathrm{i}\tilde{g}=4\pi\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}}{r}\right)\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\zeta}}{\nu}+E_{2}\right)

with
E_{2}
satisfying
|E_{2}|\lesssim Dr^{2}
. By computing
\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{\partial(\lambda,\tilde{\phi})}{\partial(u,v)}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{\partial(\lambda,\tilde{\phi})}{\partial(u,v)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\nu\tilde{\theta}-\lambda\tilde{\zeta}}{\lambda\nu}\right)\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\lambda\nu+4\pi\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\zeta}$}}\right)-\frac{\tilde{\zeta}}{r}\left(\partial_{v}\log\lambda-\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\lambda}{r}+\tilde{f}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\frac{\tilde{\zeta}\tilde{g}}{r}+\frac{4\pi\tilde{\zeta}\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}}{r^{2}}E_{2}-4\pi\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{\nu\tilde{\theta}-\lambda\tilde{\zeta}}{\lambda\nu}\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}A_{u}\phi}{r}.

Note that the introduction of
\tilde{g}
and the derivation of its desired bound are novel and crucial for the charged scenario. Next, we define

\tilde{\rho}:=r\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\lambda\nu+4\pi\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\zeta}$}}\right).

By estimating
\partial_{v}\log\lambda-\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\frac{\lambda}{r}+\tilde{f}
via (
1.70
) above, we obtain

(1.81)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\left|\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{\partial(\lambda,\tilde{\phi})}{\partial(u,v)}\right|(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v\lesssim

\displaystyle D\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\frac{|\tilde{\rho}|}{r^{3}}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v+D\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\frac{|r^{2}\tilde{g}|}{r^{3}}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle+\;D^{3}+D^{2}Y+Y\cdot A[\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu].

For the first integration on the right of (
1.81
), by conducting integration by parts and using the fact that
\tilde{\rho}/r^{2}|_{\Gamma}=0
, we derive

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\frac{|\tilde{\rho}|}{r^{3}}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int^{v_{0}}_{0}\left(\int^{v}_{0}|\tilde{\rho}/\nu|\partial_{u}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right)(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\right)\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int^{v_{0}}_{0}\frac{|\tilde{\rho}/\nu|}{r^{2}}(0,v)\mathrm{d}v-\frac{1}{2}\int^{v_{0}}_{0}\left(\int^{v}_{0}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{u}\left|\frac{\tilde{\rho}}{\nu}\right|(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\right)\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\tilde{\rho}}{\nu}\right)\right|(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

Employing

\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)=\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\nu\tilde{\phi}+rD_{u}\phi}{\nu}\right)=\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\zeta}$}}}{r}-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}\tilde{\phi}+\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\zeta}$}}}{\nu}\right)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=r\left(\frac{\partial(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger})}{\partial(u,v)}\right)-\frac{\lambda(\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2})|\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\zeta}$}}|^{2}}{r\nu(1-\mu)}+E_{10}\quad\mbox{ with }\quad|E_{10}|\lesssim D^{2}r,

through detailed computations, we unravel key cancellations in the expressions. Upon using them, we will arrive at

\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\tilde{\rho}}{\nu}\right)=4\pi r^{2}\frac{\partial(\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger})}{\partial(u,v)}+E_{11}\quad\mbox{ with }\quad|E_{11}|\lesssim D^{2}r^{2}.

Furthermore, we deduce that

(1.82)

\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\frac{|\tilde{\rho}|}{r^{3}}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v\lesssim A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu]+D^{2}.

We also employ integration by parts to treat the second integration on the right of (
1.81
). Together with
\tilde{g}|_{\Gamma}=0
, we obtain

(1.83)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\frac{|r^{2}\tilde{g}|}{r^{3}}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\partial_{u}\left(\frac{r^{2}\tilde{g}}{\nu}\right)\right|(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

Since
\tilde{g}=4\pi\im\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}}{r}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)\right)
,
to compute
\partial_{u}\left(\frac{r^{2}\tilde{g}}{\nu}\right)
, we first calculate
\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}r}{\nu}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)\right)
.Through a rigorous calculation, along with several crucial cancellations, we derive

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}r}{\nu}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)\right)=

\displaystyle r^{2}\frac{(\tilde{\zeta}/\nu-E_{2})}{\nu^{2}}\frac{\partial(\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger})}{\partial(u,v)}+r^{2}\frac{1}{\nu}\frac{\partial(\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu)}{\partial(u,v)}+R_{1}+E_{12},

where
R_{1}
representing a term that is purely real (i.e.,
\im(R_{1})=0
), and
E_{12}
satisfying
|E_{12}|\lesssim D^{2}r^{2}
.
This implies that

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\frac{r^{2}\tilde{g}}{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=4\pi\im\left(\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}r}{\nu}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=4\pi\im\left(r^{2}\frac{(\tilde{\zeta}/\nu-E_{2})}{\nu^{2}}\frac{\partial(\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger})}{\partial(u,v)}+r^{2}\frac{1}{\nu}\frac{\partial(\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu)}{\partial(u,v)}+E_{12}\right).

Going back to (
1.83
), we then deduce

(1.84)

\int_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{0})}\frac{|r^{2}\tilde{g}|}{r^{3}}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v\lesssim D\cdot A[\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}]+A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu]+D^{2}.

Plugging (
1.82
) and (
1.84
) into (
1.81
), we obtain

(1.85)

A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}]\lesssim(D+Y)A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}/\nu]+D^{2}\cdot A[\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}]+D^{2}Y+D^{3}.

We also introduce

\tilde{g}^{*}:=4\pi\im\left(\frac{\tilde{\zeta}^{\dagger}}{r}\left(\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}}{\lambda}-\frac{\tilde{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.85pt]{$\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}$}}}{\nu}\right)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\rho}^{*}:=r\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\lambda\nu+4\pi\tilde{\theta}\tilde{\zeta}^{\dagger}\right).

Here
\tilde{g}^{*}
is associated with the new complex charged scenario. Employing an analogous strategy as for
\tilde{g}
and
\tilde{\rho}
, while giving additional attention to boundary terms, we arrive at

(1.86)

\displaystyle A[\nu,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle\lesssim X\cdot TV_{(0,v_{0})}[\lambda|_{\Gamma}]+(X+D)\cdot A[\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger},\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}/\lambda]+D^{2}\cdot A[\lambda,\tilde{\phi}^{\dagger}]+D^{2}X+D^{3}.

Combining (
1.64
), (
1.66
), (
1.67
), (
1.73
), (
1.75
), (
1.76
), (
1.78
), (
1.79
), (
1.85
), (
1.86
), together with
D
and
v_{0}
being small, we conclude that
Z\lesssim D
, and inequalities in (
1.52
) and (
1.53
) all hold.

1.3.4.
C^{1}
Extension Theorem

For spherically symmetric Einstein-scalar field system, Christodoulou proved the following:

Theorem.

[
19
]
(
C^{1}
Extension Theorem with Mass Ratio
\mu
.)
Set
v_{*}>0
and assume that for every
\hat{v}\in(0,v_{*})
there is a
C^{1}
solution on
\mathcal{D}(0,\hat{v})
. Then, there exists a constant
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, such that if we have

\lim_{u\rightarrow v_{*}}\sup_{\mathcal{D}(u,v_{*})}\mu(u,v)<\varepsilon^{\prime},

then we can find a
\tilde{v}>v_{*}
and a
C^{1}
solution in the extended region
\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{v})
.

In Section
6
, we prove two independent
C^{1}
extension theorems. The first is novel, and is as follows:

Theorem.
(
C^{1}
Extension Theorem with Doppler Exponent
\gamma
.) Let
v_{*}>0
and assume for every
\hat{v}\in(0,v_{*})
, a
C^{1}
solution exists on
\mathcal{D}(0,\hat{v})
. If we have

(1.87)

\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{*})}\gamma<+\infty,

then a
C^{1}
solution can be extended to
\mathcal{D}(0,\tilde{v})
with
\tilde{v}>v_{*}
.

This is our main theorem in Section
6
and is directly related to the later instability arguments. In this paper, we also generalize the aforementioned Christodoulou’s
C^{1}
extension theorem with mass-ratio
\mu
to the charged case. It is stated and proved as Theorem
6.2
. For both proofs, we employ the quantities:

\alpha:=\partial_{v}(r\phi)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\alpha^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{\partial_{v}r}\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\partial_{v}(r\phi)}{\partial_{v}r}\right)=\frac{\partial_{v}\alpha}{\partial_{v}r}.

We further define

(1.88)

G:=\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,v_{*})}\gamma(u,v)\quad\text{ and }\quad B^{\prime}(u):=\sup_{v\in[u,v_{*}]}|\alpha^{\prime}|(u,v).

With
\alpha^{\prime}
, we then compute
\partial_{u}\alpha^{\prime}
and obtain

(1.89)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\alpha^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\frac{4\pi r(Q^{2}/r^{2}-1)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}(\partial_{v}\phi)(\partial_{u}r)}{(\partial_{v}r)^{3}(1-\mu)}-\frac{2\partial_{u}r}{r}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)\alpha^{\prime}+\frac{\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}\phi}{r\partial_{v}r}\left(\frac{5Q^{2}/r^{2}-3\mu}{1-\mu}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q(\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi))(\partial_{v}\phi)(\partial_{u}r)}{(\partial_{v}r)^{2}(1-\mu)}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\left(A_{u}\alpha^{\prime}+\frac{3Q\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}\phi}{r(1-\mu)(\partial_{v}r)}+\frac{Q\phi\partial_{u}r}{r^{2}(1-\mu)}+\frac{4\pi|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}Q\phi\partial_{u}r}{(1-\mu)(\partial_{v}r)^{2}}-\frac{4\pi\mathfrak{e}\phi\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)r\partial_{u}r}{\partial_{v}r(1-\mu)}\right).

Notice that, since
Q=0
and
\im{\phi}=0
for the uncharged case, there are only three terms on the right of (
1.89
). For the charged scenario, additional terms arise. Henceforth, we get

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(u,v)e^{\int^{u}_{0}\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}(0,v)+\sum_{i=1}^{11}I_{i}(u,v),

with
I_{i}
given in (
6.117
) via (
6.5
).
This implies

(1.90)

|\alpha^{\prime}|(u,v)\leq\sup_{\{0\}\times[0,v_{*}]}|\alpha^{\prime}|+\sum_{i=1}^{11}|I_{i}|(u^{\prime},v)du^{\prime}.

To illustrate our proof strategy, here we will focus on estimating
I_{3}
and
I_{10}
. For
I_{3}
we have

(1.91)

|I_{3}|(u,v)\leq 4\pi\int^{u}_{0}(-\partial_{u}r)\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mu}\right)\left(\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{\partial_{v}r}\right)^{3}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

Observe that, since

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\partial_{v}(r\phi)}{\partial_{v}r}-\phi\right)^{3}=3\alpha^{\prime}\frac{r^{2}(\partial_{v}\phi)^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}-\frac{3\partial_{v}r}{r}\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)^{3},

we then obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(r^{3}\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)^{3}\right)

\displaystyle=3\alpha^{\prime}r^{3}\left(\frac{r^{2}(\partial_{v}\phi)^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}\right).

Together with
r^{3}\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)^{3}|_{\Gamma}=0
, integrating the above equation with respect to
v
, we then derive

(1.92)

\displaystyle r^{3}\left(\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{\partial_{v}r}\right)^{3}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq 3B^{\prime}(u)r^{3}(u,v)\int_{u}^{v}\frac{r^{2}|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq\frac{3}{2\pi}B^{\prime}(u)r^{3}(u,v)\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}m}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3e^{G}}{2\pi(1-\mu_{*})}B^{\prime}(u)r^{3}(u,v)\int^{v}_{u}\partial_{v}m(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq\frac{3e^{G}}{\pi(1-\mu_{*})}B^{\prime}(u)r^{4}(u,v)\mu(u,v).

To prove the third inequality, we employ the equation

\partial_{u}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v)}\right)=\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v)}\left(-\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{(-\partial_{u}r)}+\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)

and its implication

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\mu(0,v)}\exp\left({\int^{u}_{0}\left(-\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{(-\partial_{u}r)}+\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\mu(0,v)}\exp\left({\int^{u}_{0}\left(\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{1-\mu_{*}}e^{G}

with
G
defined in (
1.88
).
Plugging (
1.92
) back to (
1.91
), we then obtain

|I_{3}|(u,v)\leq\frac{12e^{G}}{1-\mu_{*}}\int_{0}^{u}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)B(u^{\prime})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

For
|I_{10}|
, with our improved estimate for
Q
, we deduce

\begin{split}|I_{10}|(u,v)\lesssim&\;\mathfrak{e}\int^{u}_{0}(-\partial_{u}r)\left(\frac{|Q|}{r^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1-\mu}\right)\left(\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{\partial_{v}r}\right)(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim\frac{\mathfrak{e}e^{G}r(0,v_{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{1-\mu_{*}}\int_{0}^{u}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r^{\frac{1}{2}}}(u^{\prime},v)B(u^{\prime})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.\end{split}

Together with our treatment for all other terms from
I_{1}
to
I_{10}
, in Section
6
, we arrive at

B(u)\leq\sup_{\{0\}\times[0,v_{*}]}|\alpha^{\prime}|+C_{11}+\int_{0}^{u}\left(C_{9}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu}{1-\mu}+C_{10}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)(u^{\prime},\tilde{v})B(u^{\prime})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

Note that the exact expressions for constants
C_{9},C_{10},
and
C_{11}
are provided in Section
6
.
By Grönwall’s inequality, we then obtain

(1.93)

\displaystyle B(u)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{\{0\}\times[0,v_{*}]}|\alpha^{\prime}|+C_{11}\right)\exp\left(\int_{0}^{u}\left(C_{9}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\frac{\mu}{1-\mu}+C_{10}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)(u^{\prime},\tilde{v})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{\{0\}\times[0,v_{*}]}|\alpha^{\prime}|+C_{11}\right)\exp\left(C_{9}\gamma(u,\tilde{v})+2C_{10}r(0,\tilde{v})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{\{0\}\times[0,v_{*}]}|\alpha^{\prime}|+C_{11}\right)\exp\left(C_{9}G+2C_{10}r(0,v_{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)<\infty.

We proceed to extend the
C^{1}
solution beyond the triangular boundary. We consider the rectangular strip
U_{\xi}(u_{1})=[0,u_{1})\times[v_{*},v_{*}+\xi]
.
We also define

\overline{B^{\prime}_{\xi}}(u_{1}):=\sup_{U_{\xi}(u_{1})}(|\alpha^{\prime}|)\quad\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\quad\mathcal{D}(0,u_{1},v_{*}+\xi):=\mathcal{D}(0,u_{1},v_{*})\cup U_{\xi}(u_{1}).

Now, fix some
\hat{\xi}>0
. Repeating the proof outlined as above, we obtain

Lemma.
For any
u_{1}\in(0,v_{*})
and
\xi\in(0,\hat{\xi}]
, if
\gamma
satisfies

(1.94)

\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,u_{1},v_{*}+\xi)}\gamma\leq 3G,

then we have

(1.95)

\overline{B^{\prime}_{\xi}}(u_{1})\leq\max\{C_{12}(G,v_{*}),C_{12}(3G,v_{*}+\hat{\xi})\}=:B_{2}.

With the above lemma, we then define
