u_{2}=\sup\left\{u_{1}\in[0,v_{*}):\sup_{\mathcal{D}(0,u_{1},v_{*}+\xi)}\gamma\leq 3G\right\}.

Subsequently, we prove that for
\xi
sufficiently small,
u_{2}
has to be
v_{*}
and this will give uniform bound of
|\alpha^{\prime}|
in
\mathcal{D}(0,v_{*},v_{*}+\xi)
. To achieve this, we consider the equation

\partial_{v}\gamma(u,v)=\int_{0}^{u}(T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3})(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

with

T_{1}:=\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\left(\frac{8\pi\mathfrak{e}Q\im(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)}{1-\mu}\right),\;T_{2}:=\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\left(\frac{4\pi r(1-Q^{2}/r^{2})|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r(1-\mu)}\right),\;T_{3}:=\frac{2\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\mu-2Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right).

Employing estimates previously derived, we get

|\partial_{v}\gamma|(u,v)\leq C_{14}

with

C_{14}:=\left(\frac{4\pi\mathfrak{e}e^{4G}C_{8}^{\prime}C_{13}B_{2}}{6\sqrt{2}(1-\mu_{*})}(v_{*}+\hat{\xi})^{\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{\pi e^{4G}B_{2}^{2}}{2(1-\mu_{*})}\left(1+\frac{C_{13}^{2}(v_{*}+\hat{\xi})^{2}}{4}\right)+\frac{e^{4G}}{2(1-\mu_{*})}\left(\frac{\pi B_{2}^{2}}{12}+\frac{25C_{13}^{2}}{12}\right)\right)(v_{*}+\hat{\xi}).

For
(u,v)\in U_{\xi}(u_{2})
, we then derive

\displaystyle\gamma(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma(u,v_{*})+\int_{v}^{v_{*}}|\partial_{v}\gamma|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq G+C_{14}\xi\leq 2G,

if we pick
\xi:=\min\left\{\hat{\xi},\frac{G}{C_{14}}\right\}
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

To prove Theorem
6.2
, with
0<\delta<1
, we set

A^{\prime}(u_{1}):=\sup_{\mathcal{D}(u_{0},u_{1},v_{*})}(r^{\delta}|\alpha^{\prime}|).

Using
\mu(u,v)
being small in a region
\mathcal{D}(u_{0},u_{1},v_{*})
close to
O^{\prime}
, via detailed estimate for each
I_{i}
in (
1.90
), after conducting extensive estimates involving
Q
and
\phi
, we prove that
A^{\prime}(u_{1})\leq C_{13}
with
C_{13}
given in (
6.169
).

For all
(u,v)\in\mathcal{D}(u_{0},u_{1},v_{*})
, using the definition of
A^{\prime}(u_{1})
, we then acquire

(1.96)

\displaystyle\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq C_{14}r^{1-\delta}(u,v),\quad\mbox{ and }\quad C_{14}=\frac{C_{13}}{1-\delta}.

Applying (
1.26
), together with
m|_{\Gamma}=0
, we deduce

(1.97)

m(u,v)\leq\int^{v}_{u}\left(\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}+\frac{Q^{2}\partial_{v}r}{2r^{2}}\right)(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Using (
1.96
), we hence obtain

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\int^{v}_{u}\frac{r^{2}|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{(\partial_{v}r)^{2}}(\partial_{v}r)(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\int^{v}_{u}C_{14}^{2}r^{2-2\delta}(\partial_{v}r)(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}=\frac{2\pi C_{14}^{2}}{3-2\delta}r^{3-2\delta}(u,v).

At the same time, when
0<\chi<\delta<1
, the term involving
Q
in (
1.97
) also obeys

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}\frac{Q^{2}\partial_{v}r}{2r^{2}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{6}^{2}}{2}\int^{v}_{u}r^{1-\chi}(\partial_{v}r)(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}=\frac{C_{6}^{2}}{2(2-\chi)}r^{2-\chi}(u,v)

with
C_{6}
given in (
6.10
). If we further restrict our choice of
\delta
to
\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right]
, we then get

m(u,v)\leq\frac{C_{15}}{2}r^{2-\chi}(u,v)\mbox{ with }C_{15}(\delta,\chi):=\frac{4\pi C_{14}^{2}}{3-2\delta}r^{1-2\delta+\chi}(0,v_{*})+\frac{C_{6}^{2}}{(2-\chi)}.

Consequently, this implies the following improved estimate for
\mu
:

\mu(u,v)\leq C_{15}r^{1-\chi}(u,v).

With these enhanced bounds, we then employ the estimates for
I_{i}
in

|\alpha^{\prime}(u,v)|\leq\sup_{\{u_{0}\}\times[u_{0},v_{*}]}(|\alpha^{\prime}|)+\sum_{i=1}^{11}|I_{i}|(u,u_{0},v)

and obtain a uniform bound for
|\alpha^{\prime}(u,v)|
. This in turn implies that the solution extends to
\mathcal{D}(0,v_{*}).

In a similar fashion, we can extend the
C^{1}
solution beyond the triangular boundary as follows. Consider a thin rectangle region

U_{\xi}(u_{1})=[u_{0},u_{1})\times[v_{*},v_{*}+\xi].

We then derive uniform bounds for quantities

\overline{A^{\prime}_{\xi}}(u_{1}):=\sup_{U_{\xi}(u_{1})}(r^{\delta}|\alpha^{\prime}|)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad A^{\prime}_{*}:=\sup_{u_{1}\in(u_{0},v_{*})}A^{\prime}(u_{1}).

With
0<\delta<1
, we arrive at

\sup_{\mathcal{D}(u_{0},u_{1},v_{*}+\xi)}(r^{\delta}|\alpha^{\prime}|)\leq\max\{A^{\prime}(u_{1}),\overline{A^{\prime}_{\xi}}(u_{1})\}\leq\max\{A^{\prime}_{*},\overline{A^{\prime}_{\xi}}(u_{1})\}.

By replacing
A^{\prime}(u_{1})
with
\max\{A^{\prime}_{*},\overline{A^{\prime}_{\xi}}(u_{1})\}
, we also obtain an analogous bound for
\overline{A^{\prime}_{\xi}}(u_{1})
. As explained before, we then employ improved estimates for
m(u,v)
and
\mu(u,v)
, leading to the derivation of the uniform bound for
|\alpha^{\prime}(u,v)|
in the rectangular region
U_{\xi}(u_{1})
. The desired conclusion then follows.

1.3.5.
First Instability Theorem

In this paper, to explore the blueshift effect, we define the quantities:

(1.98)

\begin{split}\gamma(u,v):=&\int^{u}_{0}\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime},\quad\quad\gamma_{0}(u):=\int^{u}_{0}\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v_{0})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime},\\
\gamma_{s}(u,v):=&\int^{u}_{0}\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime},\quad\quad\gamma_{s,0}(u):=\int^{u}_{0}\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v_{0})\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.\end{split}

Note that
\gamma_{s}
was introduced by Christodoulou in
[
21
]
. For our use, with
\overline{u}\in[0,v_{0})
we also set

(1.99)

\gamma(u,v;\overline{u})=\tilde{\gamma}(u,v):=\int^{u}_{\overline{u}}\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

The following two properties reveal that the quantity
\gamma
is the source of the blueshift effect:

•

Property 1

(\partial_{v}r)(u,v)=(\partial_{v}r)(0,v)e^{-\gamma(u,v)},

•

Property 2

\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v)}\leq\frac{1}{1-\mu(0,v)}e^{\gamma(u,v)}.

Hence, if
\gamma(u,v)
is large, then
\partial_{v}r(u,v)
would be close to
0
.

The first property above is obtained from integrating

\partial_{u}\log\left(\partial_{v}r\right)=\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right).

The second property is a result of considering

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v)}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v)}\left(-\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{(-\partial_{u}r)}+\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right),

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\mu(0,v)}\exp\left({\int^{u}_{0}\left(-\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{(-\partial_{u}r)}+\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\mu(0,v)}\exp\left({\int^{u}_{0}\left(\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right)(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}\right).

In the subsequent portions of that section, we will select a sequence of
u_{n}\in[u_{2},v_{0})
and determine corresponding values of
v(u_{n})\in[v_{0},v_{0}+\tilde{\varepsilon}]
, where
0<\tilde{\varepsilon}<1
. This selection ensures that for all
u\in[u_{2},u_{n}]
, the following inequality holds:

\delta(u,v(u_{n}))\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u,v(u_{n}))}\right)\leq C_{33}e^{-2\gamma_{0}(u)}

with
C_{33}
given in (
7.69
) and
\delta(u,v(u_{n}))\leq 1/2
. Then, within the considered regions, we proceed to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{7}\frac{1}{1-\mu(u,v_{0})}.

We further state an important property for the function
\delta(u,v)
in double-null coordinates.

Lemma.
For a sequence of
u_{n}
satisfying
u_{n}\in[u_{2},v_{0})
, suppose that there exist corresponding values of
v(u_{n})\in[v_{0},v_{0}+\tilde{\varepsilon}]
such that, for all
u\in[u_{2},u_{n}]
, the following inequalities hold:

(1.100)

\delta(u,v(u_{n}))\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{2(1-\mu_{0}(0))e^{-\gamma_{0}(u)}}{3},\frac{e^{-\frac{3}{2}(1+2\xi)\gamma_{0}(u)}}{2C_{34}}\right\}

and

(1.101)

\delta(u,v(u_{n}))\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u,v(u_{n}))}\right)\leq C_{33}e^{-2\gamma_{0}(u)}

with
C_{34}
,
C_{33}
given in (
7.97
), (
7.69
) and
\xi\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Then, for a fixed
v\in(v_{0},v(u_{n})]
and for any
u_{3},u_{4}\in[u_{2},u_{n}]
with
u_{3}<u_{4}
, we have

(1.102)

\delta(u_{3},v)\leq 4e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(u_{3})}{r_{0}(u_{4})}\right)+\frac{3}{2}(1+2\xi)\gamma_{0}(u_{4})}\delta(u_{4},v).

Remark 2
.

The conclusion in (
1.102
) means, along the null curve with the same
v
, the outer value of
\delta(u,v)
can be controlled by its inner value, since
(u_{4},v)
is closer to the center compared with
(u_{3},v)
. In double-null coordinates, this is new, constituting a non-trivial feature of the geometry that is crucially used in later arguments.

To prove this lemma, we first the following employ inequality:

\frac{1}{2}\delta(u,v)\leq\log\left(\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}\right)\leq\delta(u,v)

with
u\in[u_{3},u_{4}]
,
v\in(v_{0},v(u_{n})]
and
\delta(u,v(u_{n}))\leq 1/2
. We then apply

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\log\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}\left(\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}-1\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}\left(\frac{\mu}{1-\mu}-1\right)=\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}\left(\frac{1}{1-\mu}-2\right)\leq\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}\left(\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{1-\mu_{0}}-2\right).

Integrating from
v_{0}
to
v
implies that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{-\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}(u,v)}{\left(-\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{1-\mu_{0}(u)}-2\right)\log\left(\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}\right),

and

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\left(-\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u)e^{\left(\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{1-\mu_{0}(u)}-2\right)\log\left(\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}\right)}.

This further yields

\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)(u,v)-\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u)\leq\left(e^{\left(\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{1-\mu_{0}(u)}-2\right)\log\left(\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}\right)}-1\right)\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}.

With these, we derive

\displaystyle-\partial_{u}\log\left(\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r_{0}}(u,v)-\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)^{2}}(\partial_{u}r)_{0}(u)}{\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}}=\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}(u,v)-\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u)

\displaystyle\leq\left(e^{\left(\frac{3}{2}\frac{1}{1-\mu_{0}(u)}-2\right)\log\left(\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}\right)}-1\right)\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}.

Inspired by
[
21
]
, we utilize the inequality
e^{x}-1\leq x+(e-2)x^{2}
for
x\leq 1
. Denoting

\tilde{\delta}(u,v):=\log\left(\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}\right),

for all
(u,v)\in[u_{2},u_{n}]\times(v_{0},v(u_{n})]
, we then obtain

\displaystyle-\partial_{u}\tilde{\delta}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left(\frac{3}{2(1-\mu_{0}(u))}-2\right)\tilde{\delta}+(e-2)\left(\frac{3}{2(1-\mu_{0}(u))}-2\right)^{2}\tilde{\delta}^{2}\right)\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}

and

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\frac{1}{\tilde{\delta}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left(\frac{3}{2(1-\mu_{0}(u))}-2\right)\left(\frac{1}{\tilde{\delta}}\right)+(e-2)\left(\frac{3}{2(1-\mu_{0}(u))}-2\right)^{2}\right)\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}.

By employing a Grönwall-like argument, we arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma_{s,0}(u_{4})+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(0)}{r_{0}(u_{4})}\right)}}{\tilde{\delta}(u_{4},v)}-\frac{e^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma_{s,0}(u_{3})+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(0)}{r_{0}(u_{3})}\right)}}{\tilde{\delta}(u_{3},v)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{-\frac{3}{2}\gamma_{s,0}(u_{4})+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(0)}{r_{0}(u_{4})}\right)}}{2\tilde{\delta}(u_{4},v)},

which implies the above-stated lemma.

With the above preparations, we now delve into the physical arguments as outlined in
[
21
]
. Employing

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)=\left(-D_{u}\phi-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)}{r(1-\mu)}\right)+\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)\left(-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}+\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}\right),

we deduce that

\left(\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{|\partial_{v}r|}\right)(u,v)=\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)(0,v)-I(u)\right|e^{\gamma(u,v)}

with

I(u)=\int^{u}_{0}g(u^{\prime},v)e^{-f(u^{\prime},v)}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

and

\begin{split}g(u,v)=&D_{u}\phi(u,v)+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)}{r(1-\mu)}(u,v),\\
f(u,v)=&\int^{u}_{0}-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}(u^{\prime},v)+\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.\end{split}

Our to-be-established first instability theorem considers the following case:

(1.103)

\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(0):=\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)(0,0)\neq\lim_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}I(u).

For this scenario, as
u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}
, there are various cases for the behaviors of the complex-valued
I(u)
. Since
I(u)
is complex-valued, we rewrite it as

I(u)=X(u)+\mathrm{i}Y(u)

with
X(u)=\re{(I(u))}
and
Y(u)=\im{(I(u))}
. Let us denote the following quantities:

(1.104)

\displaystyle X_{-}:=\liminf_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}X(u),

\displaystyle X_{+}:=\limsup_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}X(u),

\displaystyle Y_{-}:=\liminf_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}Y(u),

\displaystyle Y_{+}:=\limsup_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}Y(u).

A priori
, as the asymptotic property of
I
is unknown, we have to consider all possible behaviors of
X
and
Y
below. For notational convenience, by using
Z
as a placeholder for either
X
or
Y
, we characterize their behavior as follows:

(1)

-\infty<Z_{-}=Z_{+}<\infty
(limit exists and it is finite),

(2)

-\infty=Z_{-}=Z_{+}
(limit exists and it is equals to
-\infty
),

(3)

Z_{-}=Z_{+}=\infty
(limit exists and it is equals to
+\infty
),

(4)

-\infty<Z_{-}<Z_{+}<\infty
(limit does not exist but
Z_{-}
and
Z_{+}
are bounded),

(5)

-\infty<Z_{-}<Z_{+}=\infty
(limit does not exist),

(6)

-\infty=Z_{-}<Z_{+}<\infty
(limit does not exist),

(7)

-\infty=Z_{-}<Z_{+}=\infty
(limit does not exist).

With respect to
X
and
Y
, there are in total
7\times 7=49
cases, and we denote them as ordered integer pairs
(a,b)
with
1\leq a,b\leq 7
. For simplicity of demonstration, we focus on the
(1,1)
case here. Since both limits for
X
and
Y
exist, the limit for the complex-valued
I
does exist. By the hypothesis stated in (
1.103
), we set

(1.105)

h:=\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(0)-l\right|\neq 0\quad\mbox{ with }\quad l=\lim_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}I(u).

For
u_{*}
sufficiently close to
v_{0}
and for all
u\in[u_{*},v_{0})
, we then have

|I(u)-l|\leq\frac{2h}{3}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u)

\displaystyle=\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(0)-l+l-I(u)\right|e^{\gamma_{0}(u)}\geq\left|\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(0)-l\right|-|l-I(u)|\right|e^{\gamma_{0}(u)}\geq\frac{h}{3}e^{\gamma_{0}(u)}.

Let us define the following quantities:

\Upxi(u,v):=\frac{r(u,v)}{r_{0}(u)}\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)(u,v)-\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u)

and

\psi(u,v):=e^{-\gamma_{0}(u)}\Upxi(u,v).

We first assume that, with
n\in\mathbb{N}
being sufficiently large, we can work under the condition

(1.106)

\sup_{v^{\prime}\in[v_{0},v(u_{n})]}|\psi(u_{n},v^{\prime})|\leq\frac{h}{6}.

Under this assumption, for each
v^{\prime}\in[v_{0},v(u_{n})]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{r(u_{n},v^{\prime})}{r_{0}(u_{n})}\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{\partial_{v}r}(u_{n},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}(u_{n},v^{\prime})\left(\frac{r(u_{n},v^{\prime})}{r_{0}(u_{n})}\right)-\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u_{n})+\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u_{n})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\left|\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}(u_{n},v^{\prime})\left(\frac{r(u_{n},v^{\prime})}{r_{0}(u_{n})}\right)-\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u_{n})\right|-\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u_{n})\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u_{n})\right|-|\psi|(u_{n},v^{\prime})e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\frac{h}{3}-\frac{h}{6}\right|e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}=\frac{h}{6}e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}.

Recalling the definition of
\eta^{*}(u,v)
, this implies:

\displaystyle\eta^{*}(u_{n},v)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\int^{v}_{v_{0}}\partial_{v}m(u_{n},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}}{r(u_{n},v)}-\frac{2}{r(u_{n},v)}\int^{v}_{v_{0}}\frac{Q^{2}(\partial_{v}r)}{2r^{2}}(u_{n},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{r(u_{n},v)}\left(\int^{v}_{v_{0}}\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}(u_{n},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2\pi(1-\mu_{0}(0))h^{2}}{27r(u_{n},v)}e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}r^{2}_{0}(u_{n})\left(\int^{v}_{v_{0}}\frac{(\partial_{v}r)(u_{n},v^{\prime})}{r^{2}(u_{n},v^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi(1-\mu_{0}(0))h^{2}}{27}e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta(u_{n},v)}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{r(u_{n},v)-r_{0}(u_{n})}{r_{0}(u_{n})}\right)

\displaystyle\geq C_{37}\delta(u_{n},v)e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}\quad\mbox{ with }\quad C_{37}:=\frac{8\pi(1-\mu_{0}(0))h^{2}}{243}.

On the other hand, assuming that there is no trapped surface formation, from (
1.48
) (and its more detailed version (
7.30
)), we obtain

\displaystyle\eta^{*}(u_{n},v)

\displaystyle\leq C_{24}\frac{\delta(u_{n},v)}{1+\delta(u_{n},v)}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u_{n},v)}\right)+C_{23}\frac{\delta(u_{n},v)r_{0}(u_{n})}{1-\sup_{{v\in[v_{0},v_{0}+\varepsilon]}}\mu(u_{n},v)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{24}\delta(u_{n},v)\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u_{n},v)}\right)+\frac{3C_{23}}{2(1-\mu_{0}(0))}\delta(u_{n},v)e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}r_{0}(u_{n})

\displaystyle\leq C_{36}\delta(u_{n},v)\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u_{n},v)}\right)+e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}r_{0}(u_{n})\right)

with

C_{36}=C_{24}+\frac{3C_{23}}{2(1-\mu_{0}(0))}.

By comparing the above two inequalities for
\eta^{*}(u_{n},v)
at
v=v(u_{n})
, a contradiction arises if

(1.107)

\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u_{n},v(u_{n}))}\right)e^{-\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}+r_{0}(u_{n})<\frac{C_{37}}{C_{36}}.

This means that if (
1.107
) is true, the assumption of no trapped surface is violated, and the formation of trapped surface is guaranteed.

Remark 3
.

It is crucial to have the additional
r_{0}(u_{n})
as in (
1.48
) in the expression of the trapped surface formation criterion. By picking
n
sufficiently large, we can set

r_{0}(u_{n})\leq\frac{C_{37}}{4C_{36}}.

At the same time, for a sufficiently large
n
, we choose
v(u_{n})
such that

\delta(u_{n},v(u_{n}))=e^{-\frac{C_{37}}{4C_{36}}e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}},

hence verifying (
1.107
), ultimately concluding the proof of the first instability theorem. If we cannot obtain
r_{0}(u_{n})
in (
1.48
) and relax the factor to
1
, we would enter the realm of comparing constant with both
C_{37}
and
C_{36}
being of size 1. In this case, the validation of (
1.107
) remains open, and we would fail to provide the integrated proof as stated above.

Recall the assumption (
1.106
) we imposed. The next lemma verifies it.

Lemma.
Suppose that on top of having (
1.100
) and (
1.101
), we demand that

\delta(u,v(u_{n}))\leq\min\left\{\left(\frac{\log(3)}{8C_{48}}\right)^{2}e^{-(9+6\xi)\gamma_{0}(u)},\left(\frac{h}{1296C_{45}}\right)^{2}e^{-\left(\frac{15}{2}+3\xi\right)\gamma_{0}(u)},\left(\frac{h}{1296bC_{49}}\right)^{2}e^{-\frac{19}{2}\gamma_{0}(u)}\right\}

also holds for all
u\in[u_{2},u_{n}]
. Then, the following requirement for initial data:

(1.108)

\sup_{v\in[v_{0},v(u_{n})]}|\psi|(u_{2},v)\leq\frac{h}{54}

implies that

\sup_{v^{\prime}\in[v_{0},v(u_{n})]}|\psi|(u_{n},v^{\prime})\leq\frac{h}{6}.

To prove this claim, we first compute

(1.109)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\psi

\displaystyle=e^{-\gamma_{0}}J_{4}+\psi(J_{5}-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u})+e^{-\gamma_{0}}\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}J_{6}.

Here, we have

\displaystyle J_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{r_{0}}\left(-D_{u}\phi-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)}{r(1-\mu)}\right)+\left(D_{u}\phi+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)}{r(1-\mu)}\right)_{0},

\displaystyle J_{5}

\displaystyle=\partial_{u}\gamma-\partial_{u}\gamma_{0}+\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}-\left(\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0},

\displaystyle J_{6}

\displaystyle=J_{5}-\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}(A_{u}-(A_{u})_{0}).

To control
J_{4}
, we employ the following equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\left(\frac{r}{r_{0}}\left(D_{u}\phi+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q\phi(\partial_{u}r)}{r(1-\mu)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{0}}\left((-\partial_{u}r)\partial_{v}\phi+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q(\partial_{u}r)(\partial_{v}r)\phi}{r(1-\mu)}+(4\pi\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}^{2})\frac{\phi(-\partial_{u}r)r^{2}\im{(\phi^{\dagger}\partial_{v}\phi)}}{1-\mu}+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Q(\partial_{v}\phi)(\partial_{u}r)}{1-\mu}+4\pi\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}\frac{Qr|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}(\partial_{u}r)\phi}{\partial_{v}r(1-\mu)}\right).

In the subsequent detailed proofs, we bound each term on the right. In one of the upcoming steps, we employ the following estimate:

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{v_{0}}r^{2}|\phi|^{4}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq

\displaystyle 8C_{35}^{4}\int^{v}_{v_{0}}r^{2}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int^{v}_{v_{0}}r^{2}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v)}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u_{2},v)}\right)\right)^{2}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(u_{2},v)}{r(u,v)}\right)\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

We then utilize
(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu})^{\frac{1}{2}}
to balance the term in the logarithm and relate the other
(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu})^{\frac{1}{2}}
to
e^{\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}(u)}
. Henceforth, we obtain

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{v_{0}}r^{2}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v)}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u_{2},v)}\right)\right)^{2}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(u_{2},v)}{r(u,v)}\right)\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{v_{0}}\left(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\lesssim\;e^{\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}(u)}\left(\int^{v}_{v_{0}}\partial_{v}r(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\;e^{\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}(u)}\delta(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This leads to

\int^{v}_{v_{0}}r^{2}|\phi|^{4}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq C_{41}e^{\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}(u)}\delta(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\mbox{ with }\quad C_{41}\mbox{ given in }\eqref{FIT247}.

To bound the contribution from
J_{5}
, we first rewrite
J_{5}
as

J_{5}(u,v)=\int^{v}_{v_{0}}\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}+\partial_{u}\gamma\right)(u,v^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Furthermore, we can show that

\displaystyle|J_{5}|(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq C_{48}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u)e^{3\gamma_{0}(u)}\delta(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}

with
C_{48}
given in (
7.173
). This further implies

\displaystyle\int^{u}_{u_{2}}|J_{5}|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq C_{48}\int^{u}_{u_{2}}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u^{\prime})e^{3\gamma_{0}(u^{\prime})}\delta(u^{\prime},v)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{48}e^{\left(\frac{15}{4}+\frac{3}{2}\xi\right)\gamma_{0}(u)}\delta(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}\int^{u}_{u_{2}}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u^{\prime})e^{-\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(u^{\prime})}{r_{0}(u)}\right)}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 8C_{48}e^{\left(\frac{15}{4}+\frac{3}{2}\xi\right)\gamma_{0}(u)}\delta(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Note that the above lemma for
\delta(u,v)
is critically employed in the second inequality.

Back to the equation (
1.109
) for
\partial_{u}\psi
, for each
v\in[v_{0},v(u_{n})]
, we then arrive at

|\psi|(u_{n},v)\leq 3\left(\frac{h}{54}\times 3\right)=\frac{h}{6}.

We are left with the task of verifying all the assumptions for
\delta
, as summarized and listed in (
7.182
), (
7.183
), (
7.184
), (
7.188
), (
7.189
), along with the requirement on the initial data in (
1.108
). Our complete proof is presented in a later section. For instance, the verification of (
7.182
) for
\delta
can be carried out as follows:

\displaystyle\delta(u_{*},v(u_{n}))\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u_{*},v(u_{n}))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{e}\delta(u_{*},v(u_{n}))^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{e}\delta(u_{n},v(u_{n}))^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(u_{*})}{r_{0}(u_{n})}\right)+\frac{3}{4}\left(1+2\xi\right)\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}

\displaystyle=4e^{-\frac{C_{37}}{8C_{36}}e^{-\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}+\frac{3}{4}\left(1+2\xi\right)\gamma_{0}(u_{n})-1}.

By continuity of
\delta(u_{2},\cdot)
and (
7.189
), we can further verify the requirement (
1.108
):

\sup_{v\in[v_{0},v(u_{n})]}|\psi|(u_{2},v)\leq\frac{h}{54}.

The above outlines the proof for the case
(1,1)
. Analogous arguments are conducted for case
(1,4),(4,1),
and
(4,4)
.

For the remaining 45 cases, since
\limsup_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}|I(u)|=+\infty
, we define
b:[0,v_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
via

b(u):=\sup_{u^{\prime}\in[0,u]}\left(\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u^{\prime})e^{-\gamma_{0}(u^{\prime})}

and deduce that there exists an increasing sequence
u_{n}\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}
such that

b(u_{n})=\left(\frac{r|\partial_{v}\phi|}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(u^{\prime})e^{-\gamma_{0}(u^{\prime})}

with
b(u_{n})\rightarrow\infty
. Repeating the above arguments, similar to (
1.107
), we can deduce that a contradiction arises if

(1.110)

\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta(u_{n},v(u_{n}))}\right)e^{-\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}+r_{0}(u_{n})<\frac{C_{50}}{C_{36}}b(u_{n})^{2}.

In these cases, we instead pick
v(u_{n})
such that

\delta(u_{n},v(u_{n}))=e^{-b(u_{n})^{2}\frac{C_{50}}{4C_{36}}e^{\gamma_{0}(u_{n})}}.

Correspondingly, some of the required assumptions would have additional linear factors in
b(u_{n})
in their exponents. Henceforth, despite the stronger requirements, these are met through picking a value of
v(u_{n})
that is even closer to
v_{0}
as described above.

1.3.6.
Second Instability Theorem

We now investigate the scenario where

\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(0)=\lim_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}I(u).

For the Einstein-scalar field system, in
[
21
]
, Christodoulou remarked that the proof for the first instability theorem still holds (and, therefore, a similar conclusion is valid) if there exists a positive constant
p>1
such that

(1.111)

\limsup_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}\left\{\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(0)-I(u)\right|e^{\frac{1}{2}p\gamma_{0}(u)}\right\}\neq 0.

For the uncharged situation, assumption (
1.111
) can be satisfied. However, for the charged case, it is not clear whether (
1.111
) can still be imposed and verified. Here we take a different path and avoid requiring (
1.111
).

We first define

V(u):=\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}(0)-I(u)\right|,\quad v(u):=v_{0}+e^{-14\gamma_{0}(u)-\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(0)}{r_{0}(u)}\right)}.

and

g(v(u)):=\sqrt{\max\left\{V^{2}(u),e^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}(u)},e^{-\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(0)}{r_{0}(u)}\right)}\right\}}.

We also set

h^{2}(v(u)):=\frac{1}{v(u)-v_{0}}\int_{v_{0}}^{v(u)}|\psi|^{2}(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Subsequently, we will observe that the second instability theorem still holds if

\limsup_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}\frac{h(v(u))}{g(v(u))}=\infty,

indicating that an additional instability mechanism has been triggered. In particular, we allow

\lim_{u\rightarrow v_{0}^{-}}h(v(u))=0.

We first state a useful lemma below.

Lemma.
Let
\tilde{u}\in[u_{2},v_{0})
be given. Suppose there exists a corresponding value
v(\tilde{u})\in[v_{0},v_{0}+\tilde{\varepsilon}]
with
0<\tilde{\varepsilon}<1
such that, for all
u\in[u_{2},\tilde{u}]
, we have

\delta(u,v(\tilde{u}))\leq\frac{e^{-\left(\frac{15}{2}+3\xi\right)\gamma_{0}(u)}}{64C_{53}^{2}}

with
C_{53}
defined in (
8.22
).
Then, for each
u\in[u_{2},\tilde{u}]
and
v^{\prime}\in[v_{0},v(\tilde{u})]
, we have

|\tilde{\gamma}(u,v)-\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(u)|\leq 1,

where

\tilde{\gamma}(u,v):=\gamma(u,v;\bar{u})=\int_{\bar{u}}^{u}\frac{(-\partial_{u}r)}{r}\frac{\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}}{1-\mu}(u^{\prime},v)du^{\prime}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(u):=\tilde{\gamma}(u,v_{0}).

To prove this lemma, we compute
\partial_{v}\gamma
and obtain

(1.112)

|\tilde{\gamma}(u,v)-\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(u)|\leq\int^{u}_{u_{2}}\int^{v}_{v_{0}}|J_{9}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})+|J_{10}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})+|J_{11}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

with the expressions for
J_{8},J_{9},J_{10}
available in (
7.162
). By carefully estimating each of these integrals, we deduce that

(1.113)

\int^{v}_{v_{0}}|J_{8}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})+|J_{9}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})+|J_{10}|(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq C_{53}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u^{\prime})e^{3\gamma_{0}(u^{\prime})}\delta(u^{\prime},v)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This gives

\displaystyle|\tilde{\gamma}(u,v)-\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(u)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{53}e^{3\gamma_{0}(u)}\int^{u}_{u_{2}}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u^{\prime})\delta(u^{\prime},v)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{53}e^{\left(\frac{15}{4}+\frac{3\xi}{2}\right)\gamma_{0}(u)}\delta(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}\int^{u}_{u_{2}}\left(\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}\right)_{0}(u^{\prime})e^{-\frac{1}{4}\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(u_{2})}{r_{0}(u^{\prime})}\right)}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 8C_{53}e^{\left(\frac{15}{4}+\frac{3\xi}{2}\right)\gamma_{0}(u)}\delta(u,v(\tilde{u}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 1.

Applying the above lemma, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta(\tilde{u},v(\tilde{u}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}(\partial_{v}r)(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}=\frac{1}{r_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}(\partial_{v}r)(0,v^{\prime})e^{-\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})+\left(\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})-\tilde{\gamma}(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\right)-\gamma(u_{2},v^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{eC_{56}}{2r_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}e^{-\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq\frac{eC_{56}e^{-\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}}{2r_{0}(\tilde{u})}(v(\tilde{u})-v_{0})

with

C_{56}(u_{2}):=\max_{v^{\prime}\in[v_{0},v_{0}+\tilde{\varepsilon}]}e^{|\gamma|(u_{2},v^{\prime})}<+\infty.

Note that the direction of the above inequality for
\delta
is reversed as compared to (
1.102
). In (
1.102
) the outer
\delta(u_{3},v^{\prime})
is bounded by the inner
\delta(u_{4},v^{\prime})
with inflation factors.

The bulk of the arguments for that section relies on a simple inequality mentioned in Lemma
8.2
, which states that for any arbitrary measurable subsets in
\mathbb{R}
,

(1.114)

\int_{X}f|g|^{2}\geq 4\int_{X}f|h|^{2}\implies\int_{X}f|g+h|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\int_{X}f|g|^{2}\geq\int_{X}f|h|^{2}.

To proceed, we first deduce:

(1.115)

4\int_{v_{0}}^{v}\left|\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right|_{0}^{2}(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq 4(v(\tilde{u})-v_{0})V^{2}(\tilde{u})e^{2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}.

Subsequently, we will attempt to show that

(1.116)

4(v(\tilde{u})-v_{0})V^{2}(\tilde{u})e^{2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\leq\int_{v_{0}}^{v(\tilde{u})}\left|\Upxi\right|^{2}(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

To prove (
1.116
), we employ

(1.117)

\int_{v_{0}}^{v(\tilde{u})}\left|\Upxi\right|^{2}(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\geq\frac{e^{-2C_{55}}}{9}e^{2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}|\psi(0,v^{\prime})+J_{13}(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})|^{2}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

In the later sections, we will verify that

(1.118)

\int_{v_{0}}^{v(\tilde{u})}|\psi|(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\geq 4\int_{v_{0}}^{v(\tilde{u})}|J_{13}|(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Next, we define

h^{2}(v):=\frac{1}{v-v_{0}}\int_{v_{0}}^{v}|\psi|^{2}(0,v^{\prime})dv^{\prime}

and choose

h^{2}(v(\tilde{u}))\geq 144e^{2C_{55}}V^{2}(\tilde{u}).

These imply that

(1.119)

4(v(\tilde{u})-v_{0})V^{2}(\tilde{u})e^{2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\leq\frac{e^{-2C_{55}}}{36}e^{2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}|\psi|^{2}(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Stitching (
1.114
), (
1.115
), (
1.117
), (
1.118
), and (
1.119
) together, we obtain the inequality:

\int_{v_{0}}^{v(\tilde{u})}\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}+\Upxi\right|^{2}(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\geq\frac{e^{-2C_{55}}}{144}e^{2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}|\psi|^{2}(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Plugging this back to the expression for
\eta^{*}(\tilde{u},v(\tilde{u}))
, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\eta^{*}(\tilde{u},v(\tilde{u}))

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi}{r(\tilde{u},v(\tilde{u}))}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}(1-\mu)\left(\frac{r^{2}|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}\right)(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{16\pi C_{54}(1-\mu_{0}(0))}{27er(\tilde{u},v(\tilde{u}))}e^{-2\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int_{v_{0}}^{v(\tilde{u})}\left|\left(\frac{r\partial_{v}\phi}{\partial_{v}r}\right)_{0}+\Upxi\right|^{2}(\tilde{u},v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2\pi C_{54}e^{-2C_{55}}(1-\mu_{0}(0))}{729er_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}|\psi|^{2}(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

This would contradict the no trapped-surface-formation condition (
1.48
) if we choose

\displaystyle\frac{eC_{36}C_{56}}{2r_{0}(\tilde{u})}(v(\tilde{u})-v_{0})\left(r_{0}(\tilde{u})+e^{-\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{v(\tilde{u})-v_{0}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{2r_{0}(0)}{eC_{56}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(\tilde{u})}{r_{0}(0)}\right)+\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\pi C_{54}e^{-2C_{55}}(1-\mu_{0}(0))}{729er_{0}(\tilde{u})}\int^{v(\tilde{u})}_{v_{0}}|\psi|^{2}(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Therefore, to trigger the second instability mechanism, it suffices to require

\displaystyle h^{2}(v(\tilde{u}))\geq C_{61}\left(r_{0}(\tilde{u})+e^{-\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{v(\tilde{u})-v_{0}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{2r_{0}(0)}{eC_{56}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(\tilde{u})}{r_{0}(0)}\right)+\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})\right)\right)

with

C_{61}(u_{2}):=\frac{729e^{2}C_{36}C_{56}}{2\pi C_{54}e^{-2C_{55}}(1-\mu_{0}(0))}.

For the inequality

h^{2}(v(\tilde{u}))\geq 144e^{2C_{55}}V^{2}(\tilde{u})

as mentioned above, we carefully choose

v(\tilde{u})-v_{0}=e^{-A\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})-B\log\left(\frac{r_{0}(0)}{r_{0}(\tilde{u})}\right)}\quad\mbox{ with }\quad A=14\mbox{ and }B=1.

By employing a small bootstrap argument involving the use of
\partial_{u}\log\delta(u,v)
,
we ultimately satisfy all the imposed requirements and establish the second instability theorem. It is noteworthy that setting
B=1
is necessary, while
A
can be chosen to be as large as required.

1.4.
Related Works

In this section, we list some references related to our approach. In
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
, Christodoulou initiated the exploration of the gravitational collapse for the spherically symmetric Einstein-scalar field system. In a series of breakthrough works spanning four papers
[
18
]
-
[
21
]
, Christodoulou proved the weak cosmic censorship conjecture for this system. Subsequently, the proofs and the conclusions in these four papers have been revisited as follows:

-

The method used to prove trapped surface formation as in
[
18
]
is subsequently applied to the spherically symmetric Einstein-scalar field system with a positive cosmological constant in
[
24
]
by Costa, and to the Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field system within spherical symmetry in
[
6
]
by the first author and Lim.

-

Within spherical symmetry and self-similar ansatz, the construction of naked singular solutions in
[
20
]
for the Einstein-scalar field system later inspires its extension to higher dimensions by the first author and Zhang in
[
3
]
, and by Cicortas in
[
25
]
. A recent revisit of
[
20
]
by Singh is presented in
[
48
]
. Additionally, for the Einstein-Euler system, under the symmetry assumption, naked singularities are successfully constructed by Guo-Hadzic-Jang in
[
30
]
.

-

In
[
21
]
, Christodoulou presented two instability theorems, consistent with his earlier established sharp trapped surface formation criterion. Building on BV regularity estimates from
[
19
]
and the blueshift effect discussed in
[
21
]
, Li-Liu
[
36
]
gave a different argument to obtain instability results with a priori estimates. Note that their instability theorem diverges from Christodoulou’s two instability theorems in
[
21
]
.

Within spherical symmetry, based on the aforementioned Christodoulou’s works, various investigations have been unfolded regarding gravitational collapse. For insights into spacelike singularities in the Einstein-scalar field system, refer to
[
5
]
by the first author and Zhang,
[
9
]
by the first author and Gajic, and
[
38
]
by Li. For the study of weak null singularity, related to the proof presented in this paper for the charged scenario, see
[
26
]
by Dafermos,
[
40
]
by Luk-Oh,
[
41
]
,
[
42
]
by Van de Moortel,
[
39
]
by Li-Van de Moortel,
[
14
]
by Cardoso-Costa-Destounis-Hintz-Jansen,
[
46
]
by Rossetti, and to the references therein. We also note a recent result
[
31
]
by Kehle and Unger investigating extremal critical collapse for the Einstein-Maxwell-Vlasov system. The approach of this current paper with double-null foliations is quite robust. The first author and collaborators are extending this approach to cosmological spacetimes and other matter models.

Beyond spherical symmetry, in a monumental work
[
23
]
Christodoulou invented the short-pulse method and proved the first trapped-surface-formation result for the Einstein vacuum equations. Subsequently, Klainerman-Rodnianski
[
32
]
simplified and expanded this work by introducing the signature for the short pulse. In
[
33
]
, Klainerman-Luk-Rodnianski further relaxed the initial requirements and provided a fully anisotropic mechanism for the formation of trapped surfaces. The first scale-critical result on trapped surface formation was obtained by the first author and Luk in
[
2
]
, opening a door to study the region near the central singularity. In
[
4
]
by the first author and in
[
10
]
by the first author and Han, emergences of (isotropic and anisotropic) apparent horizons from the center of gravitational collapse are further established. Dynamics of apparent horizon is also studied by the first author and He in
[
12
]
. In
[
1
]
, the first author introduced the signature for decay rates. Utilizing only this signature, a new concise proof for the (scale-critical) trapped surface formation criterion is provided by the first author in
[
7
]
. Later, this approach is generalized to the Einstein-Maxwell system by the first author and Athanasiou in
[
8
]
. For the Einstein-scalar field system, with singular initial data, Li-Liu proved an almost scale-critical trapped surface formation criterion in
[
37
]
. It is worth noting that the construction of the naked-singularity solutions for the Einstein vacuum equations was recently accomplished by Rodnianski-Shlapentokh-Rothman
[
45
]
and by Shlapentokh-Rothamn
[
47
]
. Based on Christodoulou’s naked singularity constructed in
[
20
]
, for the Einstein-scalar field system with no symmetric assumption, the proof of naked-singularity censoring with anisotropic apparent horizon was also recently established by the first author in
[
11
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this study, we utilize the double-null coordinate system to re-express the Einstein-Maxwell-charged-scalar-field system as presented in (
1.1
).

We say that a spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g)
is
spherically symmetric
if
SO(3)
acts on it by isometry, and the orbits of the group are (topological)
2
-dimensional spheres
\mathcal{S}
. Our spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g)
can be represented with a
2
-dimensional Penrose’s diagram, by considering the quotient manifold
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{S}
. Under spherical symmetry, we employ the following ansatz for the Lorentzian metric:

(2.1)

g=-\Omega^{2}(u,v)\;\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v+r^{2}(u,v)(\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}),

where
\Omega(u,v)
and
r(u,v)
are positive functions on
\mathcal{Q}
representing lapse and area radius respectively. Here,
u
and
v
are optical functions satisfying
g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}u=0
and
g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}v\partial_{\beta}v=0
, with their level sets corresponding to outgoing and incoming null hypersurfaces respectively. In Penrose’s diagram, these null geodesics are
45
-degree slanted. We further denote the axis of symmetry to be
\Gamma
, where the area radius function vanishes, and we represent
\Gamma
as the vertical axis. Mathematically, we express this as

(2.2)

\Gamma:=\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}\hskip 3.0pt|\hskip 3.0ptr(u,v)=0\}.

As shown in Figure
2
, we proceed to set up the coordinate system in
\mathcal{Q}
as follows.

\mathcal{O}=(0,0)
\Gamma
C_{0}^{+}
p
p_{*}
p_{0}
q
\mathcal{D}(q)
u=v
\mathcal{J}^{-}(q)

Figure 2.
The coordinate system in
\mathcal{Q}

(1)

Let
\Gamma
denote the axis of symmetry as described in (
2.2
).

(2)

For any point
p
in the causal future of
C_{0}^{+}
, there is a past-directed incoming null curve emanating from it and intersecting
C_{0}^{+}
at
p_{0}
. We then assign the
v
-coordinate of
p
to be the same as that of
p_{0}
.

(3)

For the points along
\Gamma
, by the aforementioned instruction, each point about
\mathcal{O}
is associated with a
v
-coordinate. Furthermore, we choose its
u
-coordinate along
\Gamma
to satisfy

(2.3)

\Gamma:\;u=v.

(4)

For an outgoing null curve emitting from a point
\mathcal{O}\in\Gamma
, we denote it
C_{0}^{+}
. We fix
u=0
and prescribe the coordinate parameter
v
along it. We require the point
\mathcal{O}
to have the coordinate
(0,0)
.

(5)

Back to the point
p
, there is a past-directed outgoing null curve emanating from
p
and intersecting
\Gamma
at
p_{*}
. We proceed to assign the
u
-coordinate of
p
to be equal to that of
p_{*}
.

With the above-chosen coordinate system, we define some commonly used terminologies below.

•

With
q=(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
, we denote
\mathcal{D}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
to be its
domain of dependence determined by the point
q=(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
and we have

(2.4)

\mathcal{D}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})=\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}:u\in[u^{\prime},v^{\prime}),v\in(u,v^{\prime}]\}.

•

With
q=(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
, we set
\mathcal{J}^{-}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
to be its
causal past
and the following holds:

(2.5)

\mathcal{J}^{-}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})=\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}:u\in[0,u^{\prime}],v\in(u,v^{\prime})\}.

We further eliminate the gauge freedom of
v
for our double-null coordinates. Along
C_{0}^{+}
, we require our coordinate parameter
v
to satisfy

(2.6)

C_{0}^{+}:\;v=2r

and we also impose

(2.7)

\Omega(0,0)=1.

Next, we remark that the system considered above is subjected to the following boundary conditions along
\Gamma
:

(1)

For every
(u,u)\in\Gamma
with
u\geq 0
and
F\in C^{1}(\mathcal{Q})
, we first define

(2.8)

F|_{\Gamma}(u):=\lim_{v\rightarrow u^{+}}F(u,v).

Under spherical symmetry, when we consider points infinitesimally close to the center, we note that the incoming null geodesics would become outgoing in the opposite direction (and vice versa) after bypassing
\Gamma
. Mathematically, for
u\geq 0
and
F\in C^{1}(\mathcal{Q})
, we hence have

(2.9)

F|_{\Gamma}(u)=\lim_{\tilde{u}\rightarrow u^{-}}F(\tilde{u},u)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\partial_{v}F|_{\Gamma}(u)=-\partial_{u}F|_{\Gamma}(u).

(2)

As employed in
[
19
]
, at regular points along
\Gamma
, we impose the following boundary conditions:

(2.10)

|g_{\mathcal{Q}}(\nabla r,\nabla r)|<+\infty,\quad|\phi|_{\Gamma}<+\infty,\quad|F_{uv}|_{\Gamma}<+\infty.

These imply

(2.11)

m|_{\Gamma}=0,\quad r\phi|_{\Gamma}=0,\quad Q|_{\Gamma}=0.

An alternative approach to observe that (
2.11
) must hold at regular points along
\Gamma
is to consider the Kretschmann scalar of this system. To avoid being a singular point, (
2.11
) must hold.

(3)

In addition, it is worth noting that the electromagnetic potential
A_{u}
enjoys a gauge freedom as described in (
1.9
). Considering (
1.10
), without loss of generality, we set

(2.12)

A_{u}|_{\Gamma}=0.

(4)

By the spherical symmetry assumption, for points infinitesimally close to the center, its incoming null geodesics essentially become outgoing in the opposite direction. Mathematically, for all
u\geq 0
, we have

(2.13)

\partial_{v}r|_{\Gamma}=-\partial_{u}r|_{\Gamma}.

For the study of gravitational collapse, it is natural to assume that there is no anti-trapped surface along
C_{0}^{+}
, that is,
\partial_{u}r(0,v)<0
for all
v>0
. By (
1.13
), this in turn implies that

(2.14)

\partial_{u}r(u,v)<0

for all
(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Next, we define the regular region
\mathcal{R}
, the apparent horizon
\mathcal{A}
, and the trapped region
\mathcal{T}
below.

•

The
regular region

\mathcal{R}
refers to the set of points on
\mathcal{Q}
in which
\partial_{v}r>0
;

•

The
apparent horizon

\mathcal{A}
refers to the set of points on
\mathcal{Q}
, where
\partial_{v}r=0
.

•

The
trapped region

\mathcal{T}
refers to the set of points on
\mathcal{Q}
in which
\partial_{v}r<0
.

A
2
-sphere is termed a
trapped surface
if
\partial_{v}r(u,v)<0
. If
\partial_{v}r(u,v)=0
, we labelthe
2
-sphere at
(u,v)
as a
marginally outer trapped surface
(MOTS).

In view of the definitions above, using (
1.4
), we have an equivalent characterization as shown below:

Regions

Sign of
\partial_{v}r

Sign of
1-\mu

Value of
\mu

(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}

\partial_{v}r>0

1-\mu>0

\mu<1

(u,v)\in\mathcal{A}

\partial_{v}r=0

1-\mu=0

\mu=1

(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}

\partial_{v}r<0

1-\mu<0

\mu>1

Related to the gauge fixing along
C_{0}^{+}
, since we set
v=2r
along
C_{0}^{+}
, we have
\partial_{v}r|_{C_{0}^{+}}=1/2
. Referring to the table above, we note that neither the trapped surface nor the MOTS appears along
C_{0}^{+}
. In addition, we introduce the term

(2.15)

\mu_{*}:=\sup_{v>0}\mu(0,v)

which lies in the range
[0,1)
.

We further record a proposition that will be commonly referenced in subsequent sections.

Proposition 2.1
.

Along any future-directed outgoing null curve with
u>0
, if
(u,v_{1})\in\mathcal{A}
, then for all
(u,v)
with
v\geq v_{1}
, we have
(u,v)\in\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{T}
. Moreover, if
(u,v_{1})\in\mathcal{T}
, then for all
(u,v)
with
v\geq v_{1}
, we have
(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

From (
1.14
), we see that
\partial_{v}(\Omega^{-2}\partial_{v}r)\leq 0
. For all
v\geq v_{1}
, this implies

(2.16)

\Omega^{-2}\partial_{v}r(u,v)\leq\Omega^{-2}\partial_{v}r(u,v_{1}).

Thus, if
(u,v_{1})\in\mathcal{A}
with
\partial_{v}r(u,v_{1})=0
, the above yields that
\Omega^{-2}\partial_{v}r(u,v)\leq 0
. In particular, we have
\partial_{v}r(u,v)\leq 0
since
\Omega^{2}>0
for all
(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}
, and thus
(u,v)\in\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{T}
. Similarly, if
(u,v_{1})\in\mathcal{T}
with
\partial_{v}r(u,v_{1})<0
, for
v\geq v_{1}
, we then conclude
\partial_{v}r(u,v)<0
and thus
(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}
.
∎

Intuitively, this implies that once we encounter a MOTS along a fixed future-directed outgoing null curve at some
(u,v_{1})
, then the future of this curve would either be part of the apparent horizon or be within the trapped region.

Last but not least, we review some preliminaries for the (future) singular boundary of the spacetime.

In view of Theorem
6.2
, there exists a point
P=(0,v_{0})
on
C_{0}^{+}
, such that the incoming null curve
C_{0}^{-}

(2.17)

\mbox{with }C_{0}^{-}:=\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}:v=v_{0}\text{ and }0\leq u\leq v_{0}\}

cannot terminate at a regular vertex on
\Gamma
. Denote this vertex to be
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=(v_{0},v_{0})
. In addition, we call
P
as the
maximal sphere of development
, and we say that the past of
C_{0}^{-}
is a
terminal indecomposable past set
in the terminology of
[
29
]
. The vertex
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, also known as the unique future limit point of
\Gamma
in
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
, marks the start of the
singular boundary

\mathcal{B}
, which consists of boundary points of
\mathcal{Q}
lying in the ambient manifold
\mathbb{R}^{1+1}
. Note that
\mathcal{B}
contains two components, the central component
\mathcal{B}_{0}
(composed by possibly empty null segment and its past endpoint
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
) and the noncentral component
\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}
(being achronal, with
r
vanishing on it). According to
[
35
]
, we can decompose the null segment
\mathcal{B}_{0}
as
\mathcal{B}_{0}=\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\Gamma}\cup\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}\cup\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\Gamma}
. Moreover, the radius function
r
extends continuously to zero on
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\Gamma}
, but extends continuously to possibly non-zero values on
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{A}
necessarily issues from
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}
, and in particular, from
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. The relative positions of the aforementioned points are illustrated in Figure
3
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a subset of the regular region
\mathcal{R}
. With regards to the regularity of solutions to the spherically symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field system, we first define the notion of a
C^{1}
solution as follows:

Definition 2.2
.

A solution to equations (
1.11
)-(
1.18
) is called a
C^{1}
solution in
\mathcal{U}
if

\inf_{\mathcal{U}}\partial_{u}r>-\infty

and
\partial_{v}r,\partial_{u}r,\phi,r\partial_{v}\phi,r\partial_{u}\phi,Q/r
are
C^{1}
functions on
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
.

Remark 4
.

Contrasting with the corresponding definitions in
[
19
]
, here we also require
Q/r
being
C^{1}
on
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
. This follows from considering (
1.17
), (
1.18
) and the fact that
\partial_{v}r,\partial_{u}r,\phi,r\partial_{v}\phi,r\partial_{u}\phi
being
C^{1}
on
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
. Furthermore, note that
Q/r
is a dimensionless quantity, which is consistent with the other terms mentioned above.

Remark 5
.

In this paper, when we refer to
C^{1}
initial data, we mean

\alpha:=\frac{\partial_{v}(r\phi)}{\partial_{v}r}
is
C^{1}
along
C_{0}^{+}
.

We proceed to define the notion of a solution of bounded variation in
\mathcal{U}
as in
[
19
]
below:

Definition 2.3
.

A solution to equations (
1.11
)-(
1.18
) is called a solution of bounded variation in
\mathcal{U}
if

(2.18)

\inf_{\mathcal{U}}\partial_{u}r>-\infty,

and

(1)

\partial_{v}r
is of bounded variation along each outgoing null curve and
\partial_{u}r
is of bounded variation along each incoming null curve;

(2)

For each
p
with
(p,p)\in\Gamma
, we have

(2.19)

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}(\partial_{v}r+\partial_{u}r)(a,a+\varepsilon)=0;

(3)

\phi
is an absolutely continuous function on each outgoing null curve and on each incoming null curve;

(4)

For each
p
with
(p,p)\in\Gamma
, the following conditions hold:

(2.20)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{0<\delta\leq\varepsilon}TV_{\{p-\delta\}\times(p-\delta,p)}[\phi]=0,\qquad\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{0\leq\delta<\varepsilon}TV_{(p-\varepsilon,p-\delta)\times\{p-\delta\}}[\phi]=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{0<\delta\leq\varepsilon}TV_{(p,p+\delta)\times\{p+\delta\}}[\phi]=0,\qquad\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{0\leq\delta<\varepsilon}TV_{\{p+\delta\}\times(p+\delta,p+\varepsilon)}[\phi]=0;

(5)

r\partial_{v}\phi
is of bounded variation on each outgoing null curve, and
r\partial_{u}\phi
is of bounded variation on each incoming null curve;

(6)

For each
p
with
(p,p)\in\Gamma
, we have

(2.21)

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left(\partial_{v}(r\phi)+\partial_{u}(r\phi)\right)(p,p+\varepsilon)=0.

Remark 6
.

Here, we retain the definition of a solution of bounded variation solution as in
[
19
]
. In Section
5
, we will deduce the additional total-variation estimates for the charged terms and observe that the above is a proper definition.

3.
Novel Estimates for
|Q|
and
|\phi|

We now prove several crucial estimates for
Q
and
\phi
which are employed throughout the paper. We start off with a useful lemma for
\partial_{v}r/(1-\mu)
and
\partial_{u}r/(1-\mu)
below.

Lemma 3.1
.

In the regular region
\mathcal{R}
, the function
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v)
is decreasing along any future-directed incoming null curve and the function
\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{1-\mu}
is increasing along any future-directed outgoing null curve. Furthermore, if
(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
and there exists
0\leq\overline{u}<u
such that
(u^{\prime},v)\in\mathcal{R}
for all
u^{\prime}\in[\overline{u},u]
, then we have

(3.1)

\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v)\leq\frac{\bar{B}}{(1-\overline{\mu_{*}})}(\overline{u},v)

with

(3.2)

\bar{B}(\overline{u},v):=\sup_{v^{\prime}\in(\overline{u},v]}\partial_{v}r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})\quad\text{ and }\quad\overline{\mu_{*}}(\overline{u},v):=\sup_{v^{\prime}\in(\overline{u},v]}\mu(\overline{u},v^{\prime})\in[0,1).

\mathcal{O}
\Gamma
C_{0}^{+}
p
\overline{p}
\mathcal{R}
\mathcal{T}
\mathcal{A}

Figure 4.
For a given point
p=(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
, suppose that we can find some
\overline{p}=(\overline{u},v)\in\mathcal{R}
with
0\leq\overline{u}<u
such that the incoming null curve
\overline{p}p
is in
\mathcal{R}
, then the estimates (
3.1
) holds. Here the supremums in (
3.2
) are obtained along the outgoing null curve originating from
\Gamma
which intersects with
\overline{p}
. Even though the dotted line from
\overline{p}
extends to the past along an incoming null curve may intersect
\mathcal{A}
and transits into
\mathcal{T}
before intersecting
C_{0}^{+}
, this does not affect our lemma here as it suffices to only find a point
\overline{p}
along a past-directed incoming null curve from
p
such that
\overline{p}p
is in
\mathcal{R}
. If the dotted line from
\overline{p}
does not intersect
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{T}
, we can set
\overline{u}=0
.

Proof.

We use Figure
4
to illustrate this lemma. For
(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
, we have
1-\mu>0
,
\partial_{v}r>0
and
\partial_{u}r<0
. By (
1.20
), it implies that
\partial_{u}\left(\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}\right)\geq 0
and
\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}\right)\leq 0
. Hence,
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}
is decreasing along future-directed incoming null curves, which lies in
\mathcal{R}
. Similarly, via (
1.21
), we deduce that
\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{1-\mu}
is increasing along future-directed outgoing null directions. The bound (
3.1
) then follows from the monotonicity of
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}
along incoming null directions.
∎

Next, we record a calculus lemma that would be used not only for obtaining estimates of
|\phi|
and
|Q|
, but also heavily employed throughout the paper.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any
x\in(0,1)
and
c>0
, we have

(3.3)

x^{c}\log\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\leq\frac{1}{ce}.

Proof.

Let
f(x)=x^{c}\log\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
. Observe that
f^{\prime}(x_{*})=x_{*}^{-1+c}\left(-1+c\log(\frac{1}{x_{*}})\right)=0
when
x_{*}=e^{-1/c}
. Furthermore, we have
f^{\prime\prime}(x_{*})=x_{*}^{-2+c}\left(1-2c-(1-c)c\log(\frac{1}{x_{*}})\right)=-ce^{-1+2/c}<0
for all
c>0
. This implies that
f
attains a maximum at
x_{*}=e^{-1/c}\in(0,1)
, and thus

(3.4)

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left(e^{-1/c}\right)^{c}\log\left(e^{1/c}\right)=\frac{1}{ce}

all
x\in(0,1)
.
∎

Remark 7
.

Lemma
3.2
allows us to absorb any logarithmic term
\log\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
by
x^{c}
for an arbitrarily small
c>0
with an explicit upper bound.

With the above preparations, we proceed to derive some novel estimates for
|\phi|
and
|Q|
, which will be heavily used in subsequent sections. For notational simplicity, for each
(\overline{u},v)\in\mathcal{R}
, we define

(3.5)

\overline{P}(\overline{u},v):=\sup_{v^{\prime}\in(\overline{u},v]}|\phi|(\overline{u},v^{\prime})<+\infty.

And we have

Proposition 3.3
.

For
(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
, if there exists a
0\leq\overline{u}<u
such that
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
for all
u^{\prime}\in[\overline{u},u]
and
v^{\prime}\in(u^{\prime},v]
, then for each given
\chi\in(0,1)
, we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle|\phi|(u,v)\leq\overline{P}(\overline{u},v)+\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v)}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v)}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v)}{r(u,v)}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and

(3.7)

\displaystyle|Q|(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(\overline{u},v;\chi)\cdot\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{\chi}{2}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-u)^{\frac{1}{2}}

with constant
C_{1}(\overline{u},v;\chi)
given in (
3.20
). In particular, we have

(3.8)

\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}(u,v)\leq C_{1}^{2}(\overline{u},v;\chi)\cdot\mu(u,v)\cdot r^{1-\chi}(u,v)\cdot r^{\chi}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-u).

Furthermore, if
Q
is paired with
\phi
, we obtain an improved estimate

(3.9)

|Q\phi|(u,v)\leq C_{5}(\overline{u},v;\chi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\cdot\mu(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2\xi_{1}}}\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}-\frac{1}{2\xi_{1}}(1+\xi_{2})}(u,v),

where the constant
C_{5}
(depending on
\overline{u},v,\chi,\xi_{1}
,
\xi_{2}
) is given in (
3.39
),
with any
\xi_{1}\geq 1
and
\xi_{2}\in(0,1-\chi)
.

\mathcal{O}
\Gamma
C_{0}^{+}
p
\overline{p}
\mathcal{R}
\mathcal{T}
\mathcal{A}
\overline{p}^{\prime}
p^{\prime}

Figure 5.
For a given point
p=(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
, let
\overline{p}=(\overline{u},v)
with
0\leq\overline{u}<u
be also a point in
\mathcal{R}
. Denote
p^{\prime}p
and
\overline{p}^{\prime}\overline{p}
as outgoing null curves emitting from
p^{\prime}
and
\overline{p}^{\prime}
along
\Gamma
respectively. We then have that the shaded region indicated above must be in
\mathcal{R}
. To derive pointwise estimates at
p=(u,v)
, we consider the data along
\overline{p}^{\prime}\overline{p}
as the initial data.

Remark 8
.

A direct consequence of (
3.8
) would be that, for
\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}
, it yields

(3.10)

\left(\mu-\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)(u,v)\geq\mu(u,v)\left(1-C_{1}^{2}(\overline{u},v;\chi)\cdot r^{1-\chi}(u,v)\cdot r^{\chi}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-u)\right).

Hence, we have
\mu-\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}\geq 0
if
C_{1}^{2}(\overline{u},v;\chi)\cdot r^{1-\chi}(u,v)\cdot r^{\chi}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-u)\leq 1
, which naturally holds for regions sufficiently close to
\Gamma
. The sign of
\mu-Q^{2}/r^{2}
is essential for this study as it is employed repeatedly throughout the entire paper. Furthermore, from the explicit expression of
C_{1}
provided later in (
3.20
), we can see that although
C_{1}(\overline{u},v;\chi)
is a constant depending on
\overline{u}
and
v
, it is possible to obtain an upper bound for
C_{1}
that is independent of
\overline{u}
and
v
. This will be addressed in the individual sections on a case-by-case basis.

Proof.

We begin with estimating
\phi
as follows. Fix any
(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
. By the hypothesis, there exists a
\overline{u}\in[0,u)
such that
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
for all
u^{\prime}\in[\overline{u},u]
and
v^{\prime}\in[u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]
. (This is illustrated as the shaded region in Figure
5
.) By the definition of
D_{u},
we have

\partial_{u}\phi+\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}\phi=D_{u}\phi
. This implies that

(3.11)

\partial_{u}\left(\phi e^{\int^{u}_{\overline{u}}\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}(u^{\prime},v)\;\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}\right)=e^{\int^{u}_{\overline{u}}\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}(u^{\prime},v)\;\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}}D_{u}\phi

for any
\overline{u}
. By conducting integration with respect to
u
from
\overline{u}
, we obtain a gauge invariant estimate for
\phi

(3.12)

|\phi|(u,v)\leq|\phi|(\overline{u},v)+\int^{u}_{\overline{u}}|D_{u}\phi|(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime},

where we have used the fact that
\int^{u}_{\overline{u}}\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{e}A_{u}(u^{\prime},v)\;\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}
is purely imaginary. Next, notice that (
1.20
) is equivalent to the following equation:

(3.13)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(\log\left(\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{-\partial_{u}r}.

Using (
3.12
) and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we deduce

(3.14)

\displaystyle|\phi|(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq|\phi|(\overline{u},v)+\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\int^{u}_{\overline{u}}\frac{4\pi r|D_{u}\phi|^{2}}{-\partial_{u}r}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int^{u}_{\overline{u}}\frac{-\partial_{u}r}{r}(u^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{P}(\overline{u},v)+\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v)}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v)}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v)}{r(u,v)}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

To estimate
Q
, we shall employ (
1.18
). Given any point
(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
, we perform an integration along an outgoing null curve from
\Gamma
to
(u,v)
. Together with the fact that
Q|_{\Gamma}=0
from (
2.11
) and utilizing our bounds in (
3.14
), we then derive

(3.15)

\displaystyle|Q|(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\int^{v}_{u}4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi||\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi\mathfrak{e}\int^{v}_{u}r^{2}\left(\overline{P}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})+\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle=4\pi\mathfrak{e}(Q_{1}(u,v)+Q_{2}(u,v))

with

(3.16)

\displaystyle Q_{1}(u,v)

\displaystyle:=\overline{P}(\overline{u},v)\int^{v}_{u}r^{2}|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime},

\displaystyle Q_{2}(u,v)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\int^{v}_{u}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}r^{2}|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

For
Q_{1}
, using (
3.1
), (
1.26
) and
\partial_{v}r(u,v)>0
, we obtain

(3.17)

\displaystyle Q_{1}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\overline{P}(\overline{u},v)\int^{v}_{u}r^{2}|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{P}(\overline{u},v)}{(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{r^{2}\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\overline{P}\bar{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{{(2\pi(1-\overline{\mu_{*}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot r(u,v)\cdot(v-u)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(\int^{v}_{u}\partial_{v}m(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\overline{P}\bar{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{{(4\pi(1-\overline{\mu_{*}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}}(u,v)\cdot\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)\cdot(v-u)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

To bound
Q_{2}
, we shall rely on Lemma
3.2
and we will crucially employ the structure of
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}
-terms after using the equation of
\partial_{v}m
and a Hölder’s inequality. Also noting that the terms in the arguments of the logarithm functions in (
3.16
) are greater than or equal to
1
, we then derive

(3.18)

\displaystyle Q_{2}(u,v)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\int^{v}_{u}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}r^{2}|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(8\pi^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{r^{2}\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(8\pi^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}m^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\times\left(\int^{v}_{u}r^{2}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot\left(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(16\pi^{2}e(1-\overline{\mu_{*}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)r^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)\cdot\left(\int^{v}_{u}r^{2-\chi}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot r^{\chi}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})\cdot\left(\frac{r^{\chi}(u,v^{\prime})}{r^{\chi}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(16\pi^{2}\chi e^{2}(1-\overline{\mu_{*}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{\chi}{2}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-u)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Here,
\chi\in(0,2)
is a fixed constant to be chosen.
Combining (
3.17
) and (
3.18
), we simplify (
3.15
) as

(3.19)

\displaystyle|Q|(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{\overline{P}\bar{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(4\pi(1-\overline{\mu_{*}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\frac{\bar{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(16\pi^{2}\chi e^{2}(1-\overline{\mu_{*}}))^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)(\overline{u},v)\cdot\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{\chi}{2}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-u)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=C_{1}(\overline{u},v;\chi)\cdot\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}}(u,v)\cdot r^{\frac{\chi}{2}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-u)^{\frac{1}{2}}

where

(3.20)

C_{1}(\overline{u},v;\chi):=\frac{4\pi\mathfrak{e}\bar{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(1-\overline{\mu_{*}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot\left(\frac{\overline{P}(\overline{u},v)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\frac{1}{(16\pi^{2}\chi e^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right).

In particular, (
3.19
) is equivalent to (
3.7
), and (
3.8
) follows immediately.

To derive (
3.9
), we employ a trick via considering
(Q\phi)^{\xi_{1}}
for some
\xi_{1}\geq 1
. By the fact that
Q\phi|_{\Gamma}=0
and
m|_{\Gamma}=0
, via fundamental theorem of calculus and (
1.26
), we have

(3.21)

\displaystyle|Q\phi|^{\xi_{1}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\int^{v}_{u}\xi_{1}|Q\phi|^{\xi_{1}-1}\partial_{v}(Q\phi)(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\int^{v}_{u}\xi_{1}(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{\xi_{1}+1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}-1}+|\phi|^{\xi_{1}-1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}})|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{1}\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)}(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{\xi_{1}+1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}-1}+|\phi|^{\xi_{1}-1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}})^{2}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)|\partial_{v}\phi|^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\xi_{1}\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)}(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{\xi_{1}+1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}-1}+|\phi|^{\xi_{1}-1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}})^{2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}m(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\mu(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot r(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)}(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{\xi_{1}+1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}-1}+|\phi|^{\xi_{1}-1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}})^{2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The first integral on the right can be bounded as follows

(3.22)

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)}(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{\xi_{1}+1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}-1}+|\phi|^{\xi_{1}-1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}})^{2}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 16\pi\mathfrak{e}^{2}\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}r^{2}|\phi|^{2\xi_{1}+2}|Q|^{2\xi_{1}-2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\phi|^{2\xi_{1}-2}|Q|^{2\xi_{1}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

The first term in the upper bound in (
3.22
) can be estimated in the same fashion as in (
3.18
) via using (
3.6
) and (
3.7
). Indeed, for
v^{\prime}\in(u^{\prime},v]
, using the fact that
\overline{P}(\overline{u},\cdot)
and
C_{1}(\overline{u},\cdot;\chi)
are non-decreasing,
\partial_{v}r>0
and
\mu<1
, we get

(3.23)

|\phi|(u,v^{\prime})\leq\overline{P}(\overline{u},v)+\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and

(3.24)

|Q|(u,v^{\prime})\leq C_{2}(\overline{u},v;\chi)\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}}(u,v^{\prime}),

with

(3.25)

\displaystyle C_{2}(\overline{u},v)

\displaystyle=C_{1}(\overline{u},v;\chi)\cdot r^{\frac{\chi}{2}}(\overline{u},v)\cdot(v-\overline{u})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

These in turn imply that

(3.26)

\displaystyle|\phi|^{2\xi_{1}-2}(u,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2\xi_{1}-3}(1+\overline{P}(\overline{u},v))^{2\xi_{1}-2}+\frac{1}{2\pi^{\xi_{1}-1}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}-1}\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}-1},

\displaystyle|\phi|^{2\xi_{1}+2}(u,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2\xi_{1}+1}(1+\overline{P}(\overline{u},v))^{2\xi_{1}+2}+\frac{1}{2\pi^{\xi_{1}+1}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}

by appealing to the inequality
(a+b)^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}(a^{p}+b^{p})
for
a,b\geq 0
and
p\geq 1
.

We start by dealing with the first term on the right of (
3.22
) as follows

(3.27)

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}r^{2}|\phi|^{2\xi_{1}+2}|Q|^{2\xi_{1}-2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{2}^{2\xi_{1}-2}2^{2\xi_{1}+1}(1+\overline{P}(\overline{u},v))^{2\xi_{1}+2}\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})r^{(\xi_{1}-1)\cdot\left(3-\chi\right)+2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\;\frac{C_{2}^{2\xi_{1}-2}}{2\pi^{\xi_{1}+1}}\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot r^{(\xi_{1}-1)\left(3-\chi\right)+2}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

By the monotonicity of
\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}
along future-directed incoming null directions, for any
\xi_{2}\in(0,2)
, we have

(3.28)

\displaystyle C_{2}^{2\xi_{1}-2}2^{2\xi_{1}+1}(1+\overline{P}(\overline{u},v))^{2\xi_{1}+2}\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\cdot r^{(\xi_{1}-1)\left(3-\chi\right)+2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{2}^{2\xi_{1}-2}2^{2\xi_{1}+1}(1+\overline{P}(\overline{u},v))^{2\xi_{1}+2}\frac{\bar{B}(\overline{u},v)r^{\xi_{2}}(\overline{u},v)}{(1-\overline{\mu_{*}})(\overline{u},v)}\cdot(v-u)\cdot r^{(\xi_{1}-1)(3-\chi)+2-\xi_{2}}(u,v).

By Lemma
3.2
, we further obtain

(3.29)

\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\leq\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})\left(\frac{\xi_{1}+1}{e}\right)^{\xi_{1}+1},

and

(3.30)

\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\leq r^{-\xi_{2}}(u,v^{\prime})r^{\xi_{2}}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})\left(\frac{\xi_{1}+1}{\xi_{2}e}\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}.

Consequently, the second term in (
3.27
) simplifies to

(3.31)

\displaystyle\frac{C_{2}^{2\xi_{1}-2}}{2\pi^{\xi_{1}+1}}\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}(u,v^{\prime})r^{(\xi_{1}-1)\left(3-\chi\right)+2}(u,v^{\prime})\left(\log\left(\frac{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(u,v^{\prime})}{\frac{1-\mu}{\partial_{v}r}(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\left(\log\left(\frac{r(\overline{u},v^{\prime})}{r(u,v^{\prime})}\right)\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{2}^{2\xi_{1}-2}}{2\pi^{\xi_{1}+1}}\frac{\bar{B}(\overline{u},v)r^{\xi_{2}}(\overline{u},v)}{(1-\overline{\mu_{*}})(\overline{u},v)}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}+1}{e}\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}+1}{\xi_{2}e}\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}(v-u)r^{(\xi_{1}-1)(3-\chi)+2-\xi_{2}}(u,v)

for all possible
\xi_{1}\geq 1
and
\chi\in(0,1)
. Thus, we arrive at

(3.32)

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}r^{2}|\phi|^{2\xi_{1}+2}|Q|^{2\xi_{1}-2}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq C_{3}(\overline{u},v;\chi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\cdot r^{(\xi_{1}-1)(3-\chi)+2-\xi_{2}}(u,v)

with

(3.33)

C_{3}:=C_{2}^{2\xi_{1}-2}\cdot(v-\overline{u})\frac{\bar{B}(\overline{u},v)r^{\xi_{2}}(\overline{u},v)}{(1-\overline{\mu_{*}})(\overline{u},v)}\left(2^{2\xi_{1}+1}(1+\overline{P}(\overline{u},v))^{2\xi_{1}+2}+\frac{1}{2\pi^{\xi_{1}+1}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}+1}{e}\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}+1}{\xi_{2}e}\right)^{\xi_{1}+1}\right).

Back to the second term on the right of (
3.22
), by an analogous argument, one can show that if
\xi_{2}\in(0,1-\chi)
, it then holds

(3.34)

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{1-\mu}\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\phi|^{2\xi_{1}-2}|Q|^{2\xi_{1}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\leq C_{4}(\overline{u},v;\chi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\cdot r^{\xi_{1}(3-\chi)-2-\xi_{2}}(u,v),

with

(3.35)

C_{4}:=C_{2}^{2\xi_{1}}\cdot(v-\overline{u})\frac{\bar{B}(\overline{u},v)r^{\xi_{2}}(\overline{u},v)}{(1-\overline{\mu_{*}})(\overline{u},v)}\left(2^{2\xi_{1}-3}(1+\overline{P}(\overline{u},v))^{2\xi_{1}-2}+\frac{1}{2\pi^{\xi_{1}-1}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}-1}{e}\right)^{\xi_{1}-1}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}-1}{\xi_{2}e}\right)^{\xi_{1}-1}\right).

Going back to (
3.22
), by applying (
3.32
) and (
3.34
), we also obtain

(3.36)

\displaystyle\int^{v}_{u}\frac{\partial_{v}r}{2\pi r^{2}(1-\mu)}(4\pi\mathfrak{e}r^{2}|\phi|^{\xi_{1}+1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}-1}+|\phi|^{\xi_{1}-1}|Q|^{\xi_{1}})^{2}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(16\pi\mathfrak{e}^{2}C_{3}r^{1+\chi}(\overline{u},v)+\frac{C_{4}}{\pi}\right)r^{\xi_{1}(3-\chi)-2-\xi_{2}}(u,v).

We then apply (
3.36
) to (
3.21
) and get

(3.37)

\displaystyle|Q\phi|^{\xi_{1}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(16\pi\mathfrak{e}^{2}C_{3}r^{1+\chi}(\overline{u},v)+\frac{C_{4}}{\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot r^{\xi_{1}\left(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi_{2})}(u,v),

and

(3.38)

\displaystyle|Q\phi|(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}(\overline{u},v;\chi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\cdot\mu(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2\xi_{1}}}\cdot r^{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{\chi}{2}-\frac{1}{2\xi_{1}}(1+\xi_{2})}(u,v),

with

(3.39)

C_{5}(\overline{u},v;\chi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(16\pi\mathfrak{e}^{2}C_{3}(\overline{u},v;\chi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\cdot r^{1+\chi}(\overline{u},v)+\frac{C_{4}(\overline{u},v;\chi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}{\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}}.

∎

4.
Trapped Surface Formation

In this section, we shall provide a novel proof and establish a new trapped surface formation criterion for the Einstein-matter fields. In particular, we prove a desired result for the charged case. This will play a crucial role in subsequent sections. If we set
Q\equiv 0
, in the appendix, we also give an approximately 1.5-page new proof of the sharp criterion obtained by Christodoulou for the Einstein-(real) scalar field system. Inspired by
[
18
]
, for a given coordinate patch
[u_{1},u_{2}]\times[v_{1},v_{2}]
with
u_{1}<u_{2}
and
v_{1}<v_{2}
, we denote the dimensionless radius ratio
\delta(u)
by

(4.1)

\delta(u)=\frac{r(u,v_{2})-r(u,v_{1})}{r(u,v_{1})}.

We also define the dimensionless

reduced mass ratio
as

(4.2)

\eta^{*}(u):=\frac{2\left(m(u,v_{2})-m(u,v_{1})\right)}{r(u,v_{2})}-\frac{2}{r(u,v_{2})}\int^{v_{2}}_{v_{1}}\frac{Q^{2}(\partial_{v}r)}{2r^{2}}(u,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

Note that the additional term containing
Q^{2}
is of a special form and is not from the modified Hawking mass. This additional term will play an important role in the later-established instability theorems.

\mathcal{O}
\Gamma
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
C_{0}^{+}
\mathcal{B}_{0}
\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}
v_{1}
v_{3}
\mathcal{A}
v_{2}
u=u_{*}
u=u_{0}
u=\overline{u}
R^{\prime}
R
\mathcal{O}
\Gamma
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
C_{0}^{+}
\mathcal{B}_{0}
\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{0}
v_{1}
v_{3}
\mathcal{A}
v_{2}
u=u_{*}
u=u_{0}
u=\overline{u}
R^{\prime}
R

Figure 6.
Diagram for the proof of Theorem
4.1
. Rectangles
R
and
R^{\prime}
are shaded in a lighter and a darker tone respectively and the apparent horizon
\mathcal{A}
is indicated as a dotted curve. The scenario on the left corresponds to
|\mathcal{B}_{0}|\geq v_{2}-v_{1}
, while the scenario on the right corresponds to
|\mathcal{B}_{0}|<v_{2}-v_{1}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is allowed to be the empty set.

For notational convenience, here we define some commonly used abbreviations. A subscript with
1
indicates that the quantity is evaluated at
v=v_{1}
. Similarly, a subscript with
2
indicates that the quantity is evaluated at
v=v_{2}
. For a quantity with subscript
0
, this means that it is evaluated at
u=u_{0}
. For instance, we have
\delta_{0}=\delta(u_{0})
,
m_{1}(u)=m(u,v_{1})
and
r_{2}(u)=r(u,v_{2})
. In addition, denote

(4.3)

\mu^{*}(u_{0}):=\sup_{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]}\mu(u_{0},v).

We are ready to state the below criterion, which is tailored for application in the upcoming instability arguments.

Theorem 4.1
.

(Trapped Surface Formation Criteria.)
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=(v_{1},v_{1})
be the future ending point of
\Gamma
as portrayed in Figure
6
and set
v_{2}>v_{1}
and
u_{0}<v_{1}
to be sufficiently close to
v_{1}
. Suppose that there exist
\overline{u}\in[0,u_{0})
and
v_{3}>v_{2}
such that the coordinate patch
R:=[\overline{u},u_{0}]\times[v_{1},v_{3}]
lies in the regular region
\mathcal{R}
